# Singularities of matrix semicircles

**arXiv ID**: 2607.26222v1
**Authors**: Vladislav Kargin
**Published**: 2026-07-28
**Categories**: math.OA
**Comments**: 87 pages; companion to arXiv:2604.23089
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2607.26222v1

## Abstract

Let $S=\sum_{i=1}^r A_i\otimes s_i$ be a matrix semicircular element, with Hermitian coefficients $A_i\in M_n(\mathbb{C})$ and free standard semicircular generators $s_i$. Its scalar spectral density $f$ is governed, through Speicher's equation (a matrix Dyson equation), by the completely positive covariance map $η_S(X)=\sum_i A_iXA_i$. We treat the singular regime, where the pencil $\sum_i A_i x_i$ is full but not semisimple and $f$ is unbounded at the origin, in contrast to the bounded real-analytic density of the regular case.   We prove three results. (i) The leading singularity exponent at $0$ is invariant under congruence $A_i\mapsto bA_ib^{*}$ of the pencil ($b$ invertible), and more generally under symmetric scaling of the covariance map. (ii) For binary elements ($r=2$) we obtain a complete classification: in Lancaster-Rodman canonical form every indecomposable cell is of one of three types, and $f(x)\sim c|x|^{-(n^*-1)/(n^*+1)}$ as $x\to0$ with an explicit constant $c$, where the exponent depends only on the size of the largest Jordan block (the effective chain length $n^*$) and not on the coupling. With (i) and the direct-sum behaviour, this classifies all full binary Hermitian pencils. (iii) The spectral classification is strictly coarser than the algebraic one: a Type III cell of size $2m$ with non-real $β$ and the direct sum of two Type II cells of size $m$ with parameter $|β|$ have identical scalar densities, yet their covariance maps are not symmetrically scalable; the scalar spectrum cannot detect the phase of $β$.   Each type calls for a different method: a reduction of Speicher's equation to an autonomous discrete Painlevé I (McMillan) map (Type I), a Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction at the branch point (Type II), and a gauge reduction by a diagonal unitary (Type III).

We prove three results. (i) The leading singularity exponent at0is

## §1  Introduction

Amatrix semicircular variableis a random variable of the formS=∑i=1rAi⊗siS=\sum_{i=1}^{r}A_{i}\otimes s_{i}, whereA1,…,ArA_{1},\ldots,A_{r}are
Hermitian matrices inMn​(ℂ)M_{n}(\mathbb{C})ands1,…,srs_{1},\ldots,s_{r}are free
standard semicircular variables in a non-commutative probability
space(𝒜,φ)(\mathcal{A},\varphi). Such variables arise as the
asymptotic limits of large random matrices — band matrices,
block matrices, and many structured ensembles — and their
spectral distributions encode the interaction between the
algebraic structure of the matrix coefficients and the free
probabilistic behavior of the semicircular generators.

The spectral properties ofSSare determined by itscovariance mapηS​(X)=∑iAi​X​Ai\eta_{S}(X)=\sum_{i}A_{i}XA_{i}, a
completely positive self-adjoint linear map onMn​(ℂ)M_{n}(\mathbb{C}), via
a matrix-valued quadratic equation (Speicher’s equation).

The behavior of the spectral densityfSf_{S}at the origin is governed by a sharp
dichotomy, and the analytic difficulty is concentrated on its
singular side. A companion paperKargin (2026)located the dividing line:fSf_{S}is bounded and real-analytic
at0exactly when the underlying pencil is LR-semisimple,
equivalently when the covariance map is symmetrically
DS-scalable. (HereDS-scalabilityrefers to scalability
of a single map to a doubly-stochastic normal form, a notion
distinct from the symmetric scalabilityrelationof
Definition1.1.) That theorem identifieswhichpencils are singular but is silent onwhat
kindof singularity they produce, and it is the latter question
that carries the analytic content. We take it up here. Once
LR-semisimplicity fails, the regular-point arguments that force
analyticity in the smooth case break down: the operator-valued
Cauchy transform acquires a branch point at the origin, and
locating its leading behavior becomes a problem in the local
analysis of Speicher’s equation that the implicit function
theorem no longer settles. We resolve this problem completely
for binary pencils, and we find that the singularity one
recovers retains strictly less information than the pencil that
produced it.

We assume throughout that the pencil is full, so
that the spectral distribution has no atom at zero byHoffmannet al.(2026). Under this assumption, the
singularities of the density can only be algebraic branch
points:fS​(x)∼c​|x|αf_{S}(x)\sim c\,|x|^{\alpha}asx→0x\to 0, whereα>−1\alpha>-1is a rational number (this follows from the
algebraicity of the Cauchy transform,
Proposition2.4).

Our aim is to answer two questions:
- (a)

Does the singularity type change under natural
transformations of the covariance map?
- (b)

Can we classify the singularities explicitly?

The answers lead to three main results.

## Result A: Singularity exponents are invariant
under scaling of the covariance map

## Definition 1.1.

Two covariance mapsηA\eta_{A}andηB\eta_{B}onMn​(ℂ)M_{n}(\mathbb{C})aresymmetrically scalableto each other if there exists an
invertible matrixb∈Mn​(ℂ)b\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})such thatηB​(X)=b​ηA​(b∗​X​b)​b∗\eta_{B}(X)=b\,\eta_{A}(b^{\ast}Xb)\,b^{\ast}for allX∈Mn​(ℂ)X\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C}).

This is the covariance-map analogue of the congruence
transformationAi↦b​Ai​b∗A_{i}\mapsto bA_{i}b^{\ast}at the pencil level; it
preserves the class of completely positive self-adjoint maps
and arises naturally in the theory of operator scalingGurvits (2004); Garget al.(2020).

## Theorem(A. Invariance of the leading singularity
exponent under covariance-map scaling).LetSAS_{A}andSBS_{B}be two full self-adjoint matrix semicircles,
with covariance mapsηA\eta_{A}andηB\eta_{B}.
Assume thatηA\eta_{A}andηB\eta_{B}are symmetrically scalable.
LetfAf_{A}andfBf_{B}denote the scalar densities ofSAS_{A}andSBS_{B}. Suppose thatfAf_{A}has an algebraic singularity at0with leading exponent−α-\alpha, whereα∈[0,1)\alpha\in[0,1).
ThenfBf_{B}has an algebraic singularity at0with the same
leading exponent−α-\alpha. In other words, the leading
singularity exponent at0is invariant under symmetric
scalability of covariance maps.

The proof
(Theorem3.6§3) proceeds by sandwiching the Poisson transform𝒫SB​(ε)\mathcal{P}_{S_{B}}(\varepsilon)between rescaled copies of𝒫SA​(ε)\mathcal{P}_{S_{A}}(\varepsilon), and using two Tauberian
lemmas to pass between density asymptotics and Poisson
asymptotics.

Result A answers question (a): the singularity exponent
depends on the covariance map only through its symmetric
scaling class. In particular, for Hermitian binary pencils, the
exponent depends only on the Lancaster–Rodman canonical formLancaster and Rodman (2005). This motivates computing the
exponent for each canonical cell, which is the content of
Result B.

## Result B: Classification of singularities for
binary matrix semicircles

A binary matrix semicircle hasr=2r=2generators:S=A1⊗s1+A2⊗s2S=A_{1}\otimes s_{1}+A_{2}\otimes s_{2}, and the
associated Hermitian pencilA=A1​x1+A2​x2A=A_{1}x_{1}+A_{2}x_{2}.
By the Lancaster–Rodman classificationLancaster and Rodman (2005), every such pencil is
congruent to a direct sum of indecomposable cells of
three canonical types — I, II, and III —
distinguished by whether the associated eigenvalue is
infinite, real, or a conjugate non-real pair
(see §4.1for the precise
definitions). We prove the following for cells of
sizen≥2n\geq 2(Type I, II) orm≥2m\geq 2(Type III).

matrix semicircles).LetA=A1​x1+A2​x2A=A_{1}x_{1}+A_{2}x_{2}be an indecomposable Hermitian
binary pencil in Lancaster–Rodman canonical form, and letS=A1⊗s1+A2⊗s2S=A_{1}\otimes s_{1}+A_{2}\otimes s_{2}be the associated
matrix semicircular element. Define theeffective chain

\overline{\beta}F_{m}+G_{m}&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)x_{2},\ \beta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}&2m&m&|\beta|\\

Then the spectral density satisfies(1)f​(x)=sin⁡(π/(n∗+1))n∗​π​c−2​n∗−1n∗+1​|x|−n∗−1n∗+1+o​(|x|−n∗−1n∗+1)f(x)\;=\;\frac{\sin\!\bigl(\pi/(n^{*}{+}1)\bigr)}{n^{*}\,\pi}\;c^{-\frac{2n^{*}-1}{n^{*}+1}}\;|x|^{-\frac{n^{*}-1}{n^{*}+1}}\;+\;o\!\left(|x|^{-\frac{n^{*}-1}{n^{*}+1}}\right)as|x|→0|x|\to 0.

For instance, the simplest non-trivial Type I cell (n=2n=2)
produces a cusp singularityf​(x)∼34​π​|x|−1/3f(x)\sim\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4\pi}|x|^{-1/3}(Example2.8).

Several features of this classification deserve comment.
The singularity exponent(n∗−1)/(n∗+1)(n^{*}-1)/(n^{*}+1)depends only
on the effective chain length and is independent of the
coupling parameterγ\gamma; by Result A, it is also invariant
under symmetric scaling of the covariance map. Equivalently, withd:=n∗−1d:=n^{*}-1the defect of semisimplicity (the size of the largest
Jordan block of the pencil, minus one), the exponent isd/(d+2)d/(d+2), which is the form of the Krüger–Renfrew
singularity degree (Krüger and Renfrew (2025)).111Our exponentsβk\beta_{k}are
those of(Krüger and Renfrew,2025, Lem. 3.1)up to a relabeling of indices and the sign
convention there (vi∼η−fiv_{i}\sim\eta^{-f_{i}}versusyk∼uβky_{k}\sim u^{\beta_{k}}): as
multisets{βk}={−fi}\{\beta_{k}\}=\{-f_{i}\}, the most singular value beingβ1=−(n−1)/(n+1)=−σ\beta_{1}=-(n-1)/(n+1)=-\sigma, so the leading exponent agrees with(Krüger and Renfrew,2025, Thm. 2.8).(with the algebraic defectddin the role of
their combinatorial chain length) — the defect filtered through the
quadratic term of Speicher’s equation; Remark9.2gives the underlying indefinite-metric picture.

Type I is the
special caseγ=0\gamma=0of Type II (withc=1c=1). For
Type III cells of matrix size2​m2m, the singularity is governed
by the half-size chain lengthmm, not by2​m2m; this reflects
a gauge reduction
(Theorem8.3) that identifies
the Type III scalar density with a Type II density of half the
matrix size.

By Lancaster–Rodman every binary Hermitian pencil is congruent to a direct sum of canonical cells; since the singularity at the origin is invariant under congruence (Theorem A) and, over a direct sum, is governed by the most singular summand (§4), the cell-by-cell classification of Theorem B is in fact a complete classification of singularities for full binary Hermitian pencils — the exponent of a general pencil being the largest occurring among the cells of its canonical form.

The proofs of Result B employ different techniques for each
type:
- •

Type I(§5): the
covariance map preserves the diagonal subalgebra, reducing Speicher’s equation to a discrete dynamical system — a chain of
coupled equations equivalent to the McMillan map (autonomous
discrete Painlevé I). A biquadratic integral of motion
constrains consecutive pairs, and a cancellation mechanism in
the two-step recurrence pins down the Puiseux exponents.
- •

Type II(§7.7–§7.8):
the diagonal subalgebra is no longer preserved. A rescalingWα​(u)=D​(t)​Mα​(t)​D​(t)W_{\alpha}(u)=D(t)M_{\alpha}(t)D(t)witht=u1/(n+1)t=u^{1/(n+1)}transforms Speicher’s equation into a system that is regular
att=0t=0. A Lyapunov–Schmidt reduction and the holomorphic
implicit function theorem produce a convergent expansion
ofMα​(t)M_{\alpha}(t).
- •

Type III(§8): a gauge reduction by a
diagonal unitary shows that the Type III Speicher equation with
parameterβ∈ℂ∖ℝ\beta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}decomposes into two
copies of the Type II equation with real parameter|β||\beta|.

## Result C: The spectral classification is strictly
coarser than the algebraic one

Result A shows that symmetrically scalable covariance maps
produce densities with the same singularity exponent. The
gauge reduction in the Type III analysis proves something
stronger: the densities are not merely similar butidentical. One might then ask whether equal densities
force the covariance maps to be symmetrically scalable.
The answer is no.

## Theorem(C. Spectral coincidence without algebraic
equivalence).Letβ∈ℂ∖ℝ\beta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}.
AType​III\mathrm{Type\ III}cell of size2​m2mand parameterβ\betahas the same scalar spectral density as the direct sum of two identicalType​II\mathrm{Type\ II}cells of sizemmand parameter|β||\beta|, but their covariance maps are not symmetrically scalable to
each other.

The spectral coincidence is
Theorem8.3; the algebraic
non-equivalence is Theorem8.10(with the
plane invariant of Proposition8.9).

The obstruction
is visible at the level of Hermitian Kraus planes: the Type III
planespanℝ​{A1,A2}\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{A_{1},A_{2}\}consists entirely of
invertible matrices (apart from0), while any direct sum of
Type II planes contains nonzero singular elements. Since the
property “the Hermitian Kraus plane intersects the singular
locus only at the origin” is preserved under congruence, the
two covariance maps lie in different symmetric scaling classes.

Result C shows that the Lancaster–Rodman classification of
Hermitian matrix pencils is strictly finer than what the scalar
spectral density can detect. The covariance map retains
algebraic information — specifically, the phase of the
coupling parameterβ\betain Type III cells — that is
invisible to the spectral measure. Thus, while symmetric
scaling of the covariance map cannot change the singularity type
(Result A), non-equivalent covariance maps may nonetheless
produce identical densities.

## Independent contributions and context

Although the question settled here is the natural counterpart of
the one inKargin (2026), its answer stands on its
own. We isolate four contributions, then place them against the
closest prior work.

A complete and explicit classification.For every full binary Hermitian pencil, Theorem B returns the
leading singularity exponent together with its constant in closed
form (1), the exponent read off from the
Jordan structure of the pencil through the defectd=n∗−1d=n^{*}-1. The
classification is thus an exact dictionary between the
Lancaster–Rodman canonical form of an indefinite pencil and the
local profile of the associated spectral measure—the analytic
singularity realized as an invariant of indefinite linear
algebra. At the level of the question the two papers are
complementary:Kargin (2026)decideswhena
singularity occurs, the present paper sayswhatit is, and
together they settle the local picture at the origin.

Invariance of the exponent (Theorem A).The leading exponent depends on the covariance map only through
its symmetric-scaling class; equivalently, it is constant on the
congruence orbit of the pencil. This makes the singularity degree
an invariant of the operator-scaling actionGurvits (2004); Garget al.(2020), computable from any convenient representative, and it
is the mechanism by which the cell-by-cell computation of
Theorem B extends to arbitrary pencils.

A bridge to discrete integrable systems.The Type I analysis identifies the restriction of Speicher’s
equation to the diagonal subalgebra with the autonomous discrete
Painlevé I, or McMillan, map; the Puiseux exponents are then
forced by a conserved biquadratic and a cancellation in the
associated two-step recurrence. This places the local spectral
analysis of matrix semicircles, in the most degenerate cases, in
contact with the theory of QRT maps and discrete Painlevé
equations.

A spectral phenomenon with no algebraic counterpart
(Theorem C).The scalar spectral measure cannot detect the phase of the
Type III couplingβ\beta: two covariance maps in distinct
symmetric-scaling classes produceidenticaldensities. The
Lancaster–Rodman classification is therefore strictly finer than
any invariant of the scalar spectrum, and Theorem C pins down
exactly the algebraic data the spectrum forgets. This is a
self-contained negative result, independent
ofKargin (2026).

Relation to prior work.Under a flatness, or uniform-primitivity, hypothesis on the
self-energy operator, the now-standard regularity theory of the
matrix Dyson equation forces aboundeddensity, with
non-analyticities confined to square-root edges and cubic-root
cuspsAjankiet al.(2017); Erdős (2019); Altet al.(2020). Dropping
flatness while keeping the self-energycommutative—structured matrices with a variance profile—Krüger and Renfrew (2025)introduced thesingularity degreeσ=ℓ/(ℓ+2)\sigma=\ell/(\ell+2), governed by the zero pattern of the
profileSSthrough a trichotomy: total support gives a bounded
density, support without total support a blow-up|x|−σ|x|^{-\sigma},
and absence of support an atom (hereℓ\ellis the length of the
longest increasing chain in a relation read offSS). This
trichotomy is the commutative shadow of the dichotomy organising
the present series: total support, support, and no support
correspond to LR-semisimple (the bounded analytic case
ofKargin (2026)), full but not semisimple (our
blow-up), and non-full (an atom, excluded here by fullness).

The non-commutative setting.The covariance maps studied here need not be commutative. Type I
preserves the diagonal subalgebra, so its scalar law coincides
with the variance-profile law ofS=ηS|ΔS=\eta_{S}|_{\Delta}, withSi​j=|(A1)i​j|2+|(A2)i​j|2S_{ij}=|(A_{1})_{ij}|^{2}+|(A_{2})_{ij}|^{2}; there the chain exponent(n−1)/(n+1)(n{-}1)/(n{+}1)is exactly the Krüger–Renfrew degree withℓ=n−1\ell=n-1. For Types II and III the covariance map preserves no maximal abelian
subalgebra (Lemma4.5), so their scalar laws are
variance-profile laws in no basis, the diagonal reduction
underlyingKrüger and Renfrew (2025)no longer applies, and the analysis
proceeds at the matrix level; yet the
same lawσ=d/(d+2)\sigma=d/(d+2)persists, with the combinatorialℓ\ellreplaced by the algebraic defectd=n∗−1d=n^{*}-1of the
Lancaster–Rodman form. The resulting blow-ups form an infinite
discrete family approaching, without reaching, the non-integrable
threshold|x|−1|x|^{-1}as the largest Jordan block grows—behaviour
the flatness hypothesis excludes. The sharp constant, the
scaling-invariance (Theorem A), and the spectral/algebraic gap
(Theorem C) have no counterpart inKrüger and Renfrew (2025).

The length of the paper reflects this breadth rather than
incremental elaboration. The three canonical types are not
variants of a single argument but require qualitatively different
machinery—a discrete-integrable reduction (Type I), a
Lyapunov–Schmidt analysis of a degenerate fixed-point equation
(Type II, the longest and most delicate of the three), and a
gauge reduction by a diagonal unitary (Type III)—each carried
out in full.

## Organization of the paper

The paper is divided into two parts.

Part I(§§2–4) develops the general
theory. Section2collects background material: matrix
semicircles, covariance maps, Speicher’s equation,
algebraicity of the Cauchy transform, and a motivating example
(then=2n=2cusp singularity).
Section3proves the invariance of the singularity
exponent under symmetric scaling of covariance maps (Result A)
via the Poisson comparison method. Section4analyzes
direct sums, showing that the density of a decomposable
semicircle is a weighted average of the component densities,
with the singularity governed by the most singular summand.

Part II(§§5–9) classifies the
singularities of binary matrix semicircles (Result B).
Section5treats Type I cells via the chain system and its
Painlevé structure. Sections6–7treat Type II cells
via rescaling, Lyapunov–Schmidt reduction, and the implicit function theorem.
Section8treats Type III cells via gauge reduction to Type II, and
establishes Result C. Section9assembles the cell
computations into a complete classification for arbitrary regular Hermitian binary
pencils: via congruence-invariance (Result A) and the direct-sum reduction, the leading
singularity is governed by the largest effective chain lengthN∗N^{*}of the
Lancaster–Rodman form — the density being regular at the origin exactly when the
pencil is LR-semisimple — and the geometric meaning ofN∗N^{*}is recorded there.

The appendix collects the deferred proofs: the algebraicity of the scalar Cauchy
transform (AppendixA); the two Tauberian lemmas relating Puiseux
density asymptotics to Poisson asymptotics (AppendixB); and the
computations supporting the Type II analysis (AppendixD) —
real-analyticity of the solution of Speicher’s equation in the coupling parameter, the
negative-definiteness of the Lyapunov–Schmidt linearizationℬα\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}, the
reflection identity, and the explicit shifted base point.

Part I

General principles

## §2  Matrix semicircles and algebraic singularities

Let(𝒜,φ)(\mathcal{A},\varphi)be a non-commutative probability
space, whereφ\varphiis a faithful tracial state. Self-adjoint
elementss1,…,sr∈𝒜s_{1},\ldots,s_{r}\in\mathcal{A}are calledfree standard semicircular variablesif they are freely
independent and eachsis_{i}has the semicircular distributiond​μ​(x)=12​π​4−x2​d​xd\mu(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{4-x^{2}}\,dxon[−2,2][-2,2].

## Definition 2.1.

Amatrix semicircular variableis an elementS∈Mn​(ℂ)⊗𝒜S\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathcal{A}of the form(2)S=∑i=1rAi⊗si,S=\sum_{i=1}^{r}A_{i}\otimes s_{i},

whereA1,…,Ar∈Mn​(ℂ)A_{1},\ldots,A_{r}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})are Hermitian
matrices ands1,…,sr∈𝒜s_{1},\ldots,s_{r}\in\mathcal{A}are free
standard semicircular variables.

We write𝔼=idMn​(ℂ)⊗φ:Mn​(ℂ)⊗𝒜→Mn​(ℂ)\mathbb{E}=\mathrm{id}_{M_{n}(\mathbb{C})}\otimes\varphi\colon M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathcal{A}\to M_{n}(\mathbb{C})for the conditional expectation onto the matrix subalgebra.

## Definition 2.2.

Thecovariance mapof a matrix semicircular variableS=∑i=1rAi⊗siS=\sum_{i=1}^{r}A_{i}\otimes s_{i}is the linear mapηS:Mn​(ℂ)→Mn​(ℂ)\eta_{S}\colon M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\to M_{n}(\mathbb{C})defined by(3)ηS​(X):=𝔼​[S​X​S]=∑i=1rAi​X​Ai,\eta_{S}(X):=\mathbb{E}[SXS]=\sum_{i=1}^{r}A_{i}XA_{i},

where the second equality follows fromφ​(si​sj)=δi​j\varphi(s_{i}s_{j})=\delta_{ij}(by freeness and the
normalization of the semicircular law). Since eachAiA_{i}is Hermitian,ηS\eta_{S}is a completely positive map satisfyingηS=ηS∗\eta_{S}=\eta_{S}^{*}(self-adjoint with respect to the
Hilbert–Schmidt inner product).

The spectral distribution ofSSis encoded by its Cauchy
transform. Forb∈Mn​(ℂ)b\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})such thatb⊗id𝒜−Sb\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{A}}-Sis invertible,
define(4)GS​(b)=𝔼​[(b⊗id𝒜−S)−1].G_{S}(b)=\mathbb{E}\big[(b\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{A}}-S)^{-1}\big].

In particular, forz∈ℂ+:={z:Im​z>0}z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}:=\{z:\mathrm{Im}\,z>0\},
the scalar evaluationGS​(z​I)G_{S}(zI)is well-defined. Thespectral densityofSS(when it exists) is recovered
from the Stieltjes inversion formula:(5)f​(x)=1π​limε↓0Im​[−tr⁡GS​((x+i​ε)​I)],f(x)=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\mathrm{Im}\,\big[-\operatorname{tr}\,G_{S}\big((x+i\varepsilon)I\big)\big],

wheretr\operatorname{tr}denotes the normalized trace onMn​(ℂ)M_{n}(\mathbb{C}).

The Cauchy transform can be computed from the covariance map
via the following result.

## Theorem 2.3(Speicher’s equation).

LetSSbe a matrix semicircular variable with covariance
mapηS\eta_{S}. Then for allb∈Mn​(ℂ)b\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})withIm​b≻0\mathrm{Im}\,b\succ 0, the Cauchy transformG=GS​(b)G=G_{S}(b)satisfies(6)b​G​(b)=I+ηS​(G​(b))​G​(b).b\,G(b)=I+\eta_{S}\!\big(G(b)\big)\,G(b).

FollowingHeltonet al.(2007), it is convenient to reformulate
equation (6) on the right half-plane.
Recall that a matrixW∈Mn​(ℂ)W\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})isaccretiveifRe​W:=(W+W∗)/2⪰0\mathrm{Re}\,W:=(W+W^{*})/2\succeq 0, andstrictly accretiveifRe​W≻0\mathrm{Re}\,W\succ 0. The
substitutionu=−i​zu=-izandW​(u):=i​GS​(i​u⋅I)W(u):=iG_{S}(iu\cdot I)transforms Speicher’s equation atb=i​u⋅Ib=iu\cdot Iinto(7)ηS​(W​(u))​W​(u)+u​W​(u)=I.\eta_{S}\!\big(W(u)\big)\,W(u)+u\,W(u)=I.

## Proposition 2.4(Entrywise algebraicity of the matrix Cauchy transform).

LetS=∑j=1rAj⊗sjS=\sum_{j=1}^{r}A_{j}\otimes s_{j}

be a matrix semicircular element with covariance mapη​(X)=∑j=1rAj​X​Aj∗\eta(X)=\sum_{j=1}^{r}A_{j}XA_{j}^{*}, and letG​(z):=GS​(z​In)∈Mn​(ℂ),z∈ℂ+.G(z):=G_{S}(zI_{n})\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C}),\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}.

Then for every1≤p,q≤n1\leq p,q\leq n, the scalar functionGp​q​(z)G_{pq}(z)is
algebraic overℂ​(z)\mathbb{C}(z): there exists a nonzero polynomialPp​q​(z,w)∈ℂ​[z,w]P_{pq}(z,w)\in\mathbb{C}[z,w]such thatPp​q​(z,Gp​q​(z))=0for all​z∈ℂ+.P_{pq}\bigl(z,G_{pq}(z)\bigr)=0\qquad\text{for all }z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}.

In particular,H​(z)=tr⁡G​(z)=1n​∑pGp​p​(z)H(z)=\operatorname{tr}G(z)=\tfrac{1}{n}\sum_{p}G_{pp}(z)is
algebraic overℂ​(z)\mathbb{C}(z).

For a proof see AppendixA.

## Proposition 2.5(HHhas real-coefficient minimal polynomial).

The minimal polynomial ofH​(z)=tr⁡G​(z)H(z)=\operatorname{tr}G(z)overℂ​(z)\mathbb{C}(z)has coefficients inℝ​(z)\mathbb{R}(z). Equivalently, there is
a nonzero polynomialP​(z,w)∈ℝ​[z,w]P(z,w)\in\mathbb{R}[z,w], irreducible
inℂ​[z,w]\mathbb{C}[z,w], withP​(z,H​(z))=0P(z,H(z))=0onℂ+\mathbb{C}^{+}.

## Proof.

LetP​(w)=wd+ad−1​(z)​wd−1+⋯+a0​(z)∈ℂ​(z)​[w]P(w)=w^{d}+a_{d-1}(z)w^{d-1}+\cdots+a_{0}(z)\in\mathbb{C}(z)[w]be
the minimal polynomial ofHHoverℂ​(z)\mathbb{C}(z). SinceμS\mu_{S}is a
real measure, Schwarz reflection givesH​(z)¯=H​(z¯)\overline{H(z)}=H(\overline{z})forz∈ℂ∖ℝz\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}. Conjugating the identityP​(z,H​(z))=0P(z,H(z))=0yieldsP¯​(z¯,H​(z¯))=0\overline{P}(\overline{z},H(\overline{z}))=0onℂ+\mathbb{C}^{+},
whereP¯\overline{P}denotes the polynomial obtained by conjugating the
coefficients of eachaia_{i}. By analytic continuation,P¯\overline{P}also
annihilatesHHonℂ+\mathbb{C}^{+}. SinceP¯\overline{P}is monic of
degreedd, minimality forcesP¯=P\overline{P}=P, soai∈ℝ​(z)a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}(z)for everyii. Clearing denominators gives
the claimed element ofℝ​[z,w]\mathbb{R}[z,w].
∎

## Corollary 2.6(Structure of the spectral measure).

The spectral measure decomposes asμS=f​(x)​d​x+∑j=1Nmj​δxj,N<∞,\mu_{S}\;=\;f(x)\,dx\;+\;\sum_{j=1}^{N}m_{j}\,\delta_{x_{j}},\qquad N<\infty,

where the densityffis real-analytic onℝ\mathbb{R}except
at finitely many points, at which it has either a pole
(corresponding to an atomxjx_{j}) or an algebraic singularity.

## Proof.

Standard:HHis algebraic with real-coefficient minimal
polynomial, so its singularities inℂ\mathbb{C}form a finite
set (zeros of the discriminant and leading coefficient). Real
poles ofHHcorrespond to atoms ofμS\mu_{S}via Stieltjes
inversion, with masses given by residues; real branch points
give algebraic singularities offfon each side via Puiseux
expansion. Off this finite set,HHextends analytically acrossℝ\mathbb{R}, so the singular continuous part ofμS\mu_{S}is
zero.
∎

LetW​(u)W(u)be the strictly accretive solution of (7)

In particular,W​(t)≻0W(t)\succ 0for everyt>0t>0.

For a proof see Lemma 5.1 inKargin (2026).

## Example 2.8(Cusp singularity and congruence invariance forn=2n=2).

LetS=A1⊗s1+A2⊗s2S=A_{1}\otimes s_{1}+A_{2}\otimes s_{2}withA1=(0110)A_{1}=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)andA2=(0001)A_{2}=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr).
This is the Type I Lancaster–Rodman cellS2S_{2}(up to reordering of generators);
it is the smallest cell whose density is singular at the origin, and we work it out

0&u^{1/3}-\tfrac{2}{3}\,u+O(u^{5/3})\end{pmatrix}.

Asu→0+u\to 0^{+}the(1,1)(1,1)-entry diverges and the(2,2)(2,2)-entry vanishes; in the

To pass to the density, recallGS​(z​I)=−i​W​(u)G_{S}(zI)=-\,iW(u)withz=i​uz=iu, so thatf​(x)=1π​limε↓0Im​[−tr⁡GS​((x+i​ε)​I)]=1π​Re​tr⁡W​(u),f(x)=\tfrac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\mathrm{Im}\,\bigl[-\operatorname{tr}G_{S}\bigl((x+i\varepsilon)I\bigr)\bigr]=\tfrac{1}{\pi}\,\mathrm{Re}\,\,\operatorname{tr}W(u),

evaluated atu=−i​x​(Re​u>0)u=-ix\ (\mathrm{Re}\,u>0).
The positive branch ofu−1/3u^{-1/3}is the principal one (real foru>0u>0); atu=−i​xu=-ixit equals|x|−1/3​e±i​π/6|x|^{-1/3}e^{\pm i\pi/6}forx≷0x\gtrless 0, soRe​u−1/3=cos⁡(π/6)​|x|−1/3\mathrm{Re}\,\,u^{-1/3}=\cos(\pi/6)\,|x|^{-1/3}on both sides. Therefore(9)f​(x)=cos⁡(π/6)2​π​|x|−1/3+o​(|x|−1/3)=34​π​|x|−1/3+o​(|x|−1/3),x→0.f(x)=\frac{\cos(\pi/6)}{2\pi}\,|x|^{-1/3}+o\bigl(|x|^{-1/3}\bigr)=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4\pi}\,|x|^{-1/3}+o\bigl(|x|^{-1/3}\bigr),\qquad x\to 0.

This is a cusp of exponent13=n−1n+1|n=2\tfrac{1}{3}=\frac{n-1}{n+1}\big|_{n=2}, and the constant34​π=1n​π​sin⁡πn+1|n=2\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4\pi}=\frac{1}{n\pi}\sin\frac{\pi}{n+1}\big|_{n=2}is theα=0\alpha=0specialization of the sharp constantCα,nC_{\alpha,n}of

The example also previews the behavior under congruence. The flipc=A1c=A_{1}is a

0&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr). The transformed

diagonal entries are swapped. Thus the entrywise Puiseux exponents (−13-\tfrac{1}{3}and+13+\tfrac{1}{3}) are permuted, while the trace exponent — and hence the density
exponent13\tfrac{1}{3}— is unchanged. Hereccis unitary, so density invariance is
immediate; the substantive statement, that the exponent is preserved under
congruence by anarbitrarypositivebb, is
Theorem3.6.

## §3  Invariance under symmetric covariance-map scaling

Define thePoisson indicatorofSSatx=0x=0by𝒫S​(ε):=1π​∫ℝεx2+ε2​𝑑μS​(x)=−1π​Im​H​(i​ε),ε>0,\mathcal{P}_{S}(\varepsilon)\;:=\;\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\varepsilon}{x^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}}\,d\mu_{S}(x)\;=\;-\frac{1}{\pi}\,\mathrm{Im}\,\,H(i\varepsilon),\qquad\varepsilon>0,

whereH​(z)=tr⁡GS​(z​I)H(z)=\operatorname{tr}\,G_{S}(zI)is the scalar Cauchy transform
andμS\mu_{S}is the spectral measure ofSS.

## Proposition 3.1(Poisson comparison under congruence).

LetS∈Mn​(ℂ)⊗𝒜S\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathcal{A}be a bounded self-adjoint random variable,
letb∈Mn​(ℂ)b\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})be invertible, and setT=(b⊗I)​S​(b∗⊗I),T=(b\otimes I)\,S\,(b^{*}\otimes I),

which is again self-adjoint. Then for everyε>0\varepsilon>0,σmin​(b)‖b‖3​𝒫S​(ε‖b‖​σmin​(b))≤𝒫T​(ε)≤‖b‖σmin​(b)3​𝒫S​(ε‖b‖​σmin​(b)).\frac{\sigma_{\min}(b)}{\|b\|^{3}}\,\mathcal{P}_{S}\!\Bigl(\frac{\varepsilon}{\|b\|\sigma_{\min}(b)}\Bigr)\;\leq\;\mathcal{P}_{T}(\varepsilon)\;\leq\;\frac{\|b\|}{\sigma_{\min}(b)^{3}}\,\mathcal{P}_{S}\!\Bigl(\frac{\varepsilon}{\|b\|\sigma_{\min}(b)}\Bigr).

In particular, the blow-up exponent of the Poisson transform at0is
preserved under the congruenceS↦b​S​b∗S\mapsto bSb^{\ast}.

## Proof.

SinceP∗=PP^{*}=P,T=(b⊗I)​S​(b∗⊗I)=(u⊗I)​[(P⊗I)​S​(P⊗I)]​(u∗⊗I).T=(b\otimes I)\,S\,(b^{*}\otimes I)=(u\otimes I)\,\bigl[(P\otimes I)\,S\,(P\otimes I)\bigr]\,(u^{*}\otimes I).

The Poisson indicator is invariant under conjugation by the unitaryu⊗Iu\otimes I: writingτ=tr⊗φ\tau=\operatorname{tr}\otimes\varphiandR:=(P⊗I)​S​(P⊗I)R:=(P\otimes I)S(P\otimes I),𝒫T​(ε)=επ​τ​((T2+ε2​I)−1)=επ​τ​((u⊗I)​(R2+ε2​I)−1​(u∗⊗I))=𝒫R​(ε),\mathcal{P}_{T}(\varepsilon)=\frac{\varepsilon}{\pi}\,\tau\!\bigl((T^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}I)^{-1}\bigr)=\frac{\varepsilon}{\pi}\,\tau\!\bigl((u\otimes I)(R^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}I)^{-1}(u^{*}\otimes I)\bigr)=\mathcal{P}_{R}(\varepsilon),

usingT2=(u⊗I)​R2​(u∗⊗I)T^{2}=(u\otimes I)R^{2}(u^{*}\otimes I)and the trace identityτ​((u⊗I)​Y​(u∗⊗I))=τ​(Y)\tau\bigl((u\otimes I)Y(u^{*}\otimes I)\bigr)=\tau(Y). Moreover‖P‖=‖b‖\|P\|=\|b\|andσmin​(P)=σmin​(b)\sigma_{\min}(P)=\sigma_{\min}(b). It therefore suffices to
prove the estimate for the positive congruenceR=(P⊗I)​S​(P⊗I)R=(P\otimes I)S(P\otimes I),
i.e. we may assumeb=Pb=Pis positive. We do so from now on.

LetM:=‖b‖M:=\|b\|,m:=σmin​(b)m:=\sigma_{\min}(b), whereb∈Mn​(ℂ)b\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})is positive and invertible. Thenm​I⪯b⪯M​I,m2​I⪯b2⪯M2​I,M−2​I⪯b−2⪯m−2​I.mI\preceq b\preceq MI,\qquad m^{2}I\preceq b^{2}\preceq M^{2}I,\qquad M^{-2}I\preceq b^{-2}\preceq m^{-2}I.

Writeτ:=tr⊗φ\tau:=\operatorname{tr}\otimes\varphiandT=b​S​bT=bSb.
We computeT2+α2​I=b​S​b2​S​b+α2​I=b​(S​b2​S+α2​b−2)​b,T^{2}+\alpha^{2}I=bSb^{2}Sb+\alpha^{2}I=b\bigl(Sb^{2}S+\alpha^{2}b^{-2}\bigr)b,

and therefore(10)(T2+α2​I)−1=b−1​(S​b2​S+α2​b−2)−1​b−1.(T^{2}+\alpha^{2}I)^{-1}=b^{-1}\bigl(Sb^{2}S+\alpha^{2}b^{-2}\bigr)^{-1}b^{-1}.

SinceS=S∗S=S^{\ast}, the mapX↦S​X​SX\mapsto SXSis CP. Hence fromm2​I⪯b2⪯M2​Im^{2}I\preceq b^{2}\preceq M^{2}Iwe obtainm2​S2⪯S​b2​S⪯M2​S2.m^{2}S^{2}\preceq Sb^{2}S\preceq M^{2}S^{2}.

Also,α2​M−2​I⪯α2​b−2⪯α2​m−2​I.\alpha^{2}M^{-2}I\preceq\alpha^{2}b^{-2}\preceq\alpha^{2}m^{-2}I.

andX⪰0X\succeq 0, we haveM−2​τ​(X)≤τ​(b−2​X)≤m−2​τ​(X).M^{-2}\tau(X)\leq\tau(b^{-2}X)\leq m^{-2}\tau(X).

Combining this with the operator inequality above, we obtainτ​((M2​S2+α2​m−2​I)−1)M2≤τ​((T2+α2​I)−1)≤τ​((m2​S2+α2​M−2​I)−1)m2.\frac{\tau\!\left(\bigl(M^{2}S^{2}+\alpha^{2}m^{-2}I\bigr)^{-1}\right)}{M^{2}}\;\leq\;\tau\bigl((T^{2}+\alpha^{2}I)^{-1}\bigr)\;\leq\;\frac{\tau\!\left(\bigl(m^{2}S^{2}+\alpha^{2}M^{-2}I\bigr)^{-1}\right)}{m^{2}}.

Factoring outM2M^{2}andm2m^{2}, this becomes(11)τ​((S2+α2m2​M2​I)−1)M4≤τ​((T2+α2​I)−1)≤τ​((S2+α2m2​M2​I)−1)m4.\frac{\tau\!\left(\Bigl(S^{2}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{m^{2}M^{2}}I\Bigr)^{-1}\right)}{M^{4}}\;\leq\;\tau\bigl((T^{2}+\alpha^{2}I)^{-1}\bigr)\;\leq\;\frac{\tau\!\left(\Bigl(S^{2}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{m^{2}M^{2}}I\Bigr)^{-1}\right)}{m^{4}}.

Since by definition:𝒫T​(α):=απ​τ​((T2+α2​I)−1),α>0,\mathcal{P}_{T}(\alpha):=\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\,\tau\bigl((T^{2}+\alpha^{2}I)^{-1}\bigr),\qquad\alpha>0,

then (11) can be rewritten as(12)mM3​𝒫S​(αm​M)≤𝒫T​(α)≤Mm3​𝒫S​(αm​M).\frac{m}{M^{3}}\,\mathcal{P}_{S}\!\Bigl(\frac{\alpha}{mM}\Bigr)\;\leq\;\mathcal{P}_{T}(\alpha)\;\leq\;\frac{M}{m^{3}}\,\mathcal{P}_{S}\!\Bigl(\frac{\alpha}{mM}\Bigr).

## Proposition 3.2(Poisson comparison under covariance-map scaling).

LetSA=∑iAi⊗si,SB=∑jBj⊗tjS_{A}=\sum_{i}A_{i}\otimes s_{i},\qquad S_{B}=\sum_{j}B_{j}\otimes t_{j}

be matrix semicircles, and let their covariance maps beηA​(X)=∑iAi​X​Ai∗,ηB​(X)=∑jBj​X​Bj∗.\eta_{A}(X)=\sum_{i}A_{i}XA_{i}^{*},\qquad\eta_{B}(X)=\sum_{j}B_{j}XB_{j}^{*}.

Assume that there exists an invertible matrixb∈Mn​(ℂ)b\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})such thatηB​(X)=b​ηA​(b∗​X​b)​b∗,X∈Mn​(ℂ).\eta_{B}(X)=b\,\eta_{A}(b^{*}Xb)\,b^{*},\qquad X\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C}).

Then, for everyε>0\varepsilon>0,σmin​(b)‖b‖3​𝒫SA​(ε‖b‖​σmin​(b))≤𝒫SB​(ε)≤‖b‖σmin​(b)3​𝒫SA​(ε‖b‖​σmin​(b)).\frac{\sigma_{\min}(b)}{\|b\|^{3}}\,\mathcal{P}_{S_{A}}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\|b\|\sigma_{\min}(b)}\right)\leq\mathcal{P}_{S_{B}}(\varepsilon)\leq\frac{\|b\|}{\sigma_{\min}(b)^{3}}\,\mathcal{P}_{S_{A}}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\|b\|\sigma_{\min}(b)}\right).

## Proof.

LetS~=(b⊗I)​SA​(b∗⊗I).\widetilde{S}=(b\otimes I)S_{A}(b^{*}\otimes I).

ThenS~\widetilde{S}is again a matrix semicircle. Its covariance map isηS~​(X)=b​ηA​(b∗​X​b)​b∗=ηB​(X).\eta_{\widetilde{S}}(X)=b\,\eta_{A}(b^{*}Xb)\,b^{*}=\eta_{B}(X).

Since the scalar distribution of a centered matrix semicircle is determined by
its covariance map, we haveμS~=μSB,𝒫S~​(ε)=𝒫SB​(ε).\mu_{\widetilde{S}}=\mu_{S_{B}},\qquad\mathcal{P}_{\widetilde{S}}(\varepsilon)=\mathcal{P}_{S_{B}}(\varepsilon).

Applying Proposition3.1toSAS_{A}andS~\widetilde{S}givesσmin​(b)‖b‖3​𝒫SA​(ε‖b‖​σmin​(b))≤𝒫S~​(ε)≤‖b‖σmin​(b)3​𝒫SA​(ε‖b‖​σmin​(b)).\frac{\sigma_{\min}(b)}{\|b\|^{3}}\,\mathcal{P}_{S_{A}}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\|b\|\sigma_{\min}(b)}\right)\leq\mathcal{P}_{\widetilde{S}}(\varepsilon)\leq\frac{\|b\|}{\sigma_{\min}(b)^{3}}\,\mathcal{P}_{S_{A}}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\|b\|\sigma_{\min}(b)}\right).

Replacing𝒫S~\mathcal{P}_{\widetilde{S}}by𝒫SB\mathcal{P}_{S_{B}}proves the claim.
∎

## Corollary 3.3(Preservation of the Poisson blow-up exponent under covariance-map scaling).

Assume the setting of Proposition3.2. If, for someβ∈[0,1)\beta\in[0,1), there exist constantsc1,c2,ε0>0c_{1},c_{2},\varepsilon_{0}>0such thatc1​ε−β≤𝒫SA​(ε)≤c2​ε−β,0<ε<ε0,c_{1}\,\varepsilon^{-\beta}\leq\mathcal{P}_{S_{A}}(\varepsilon)\leq c_{2}\,\varepsilon^{-\beta},\qquad 0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0},

then there exist constantsc1′,c2′,ε0′>0c_{1}^{\prime},c_{2}^{\prime},\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}>0such thatc1′​ε−β≤𝒫SB​(ε)≤c2′​ε−β,0<ε<ε0′.c_{1}^{\prime}\,\varepsilon^{-\beta}\leq\mathcal{P}_{S_{B}}(\varepsilon)\leq c_{2}^{\prime}\,\varepsilon^{-\beta},\qquad 0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}.

In particular, the Poisson blow-up exponent at0is invariant under
symmetric scalability of covariance maps.

## Proof.

LetM:=‖b‖,m:=σmin​(b).M:=\|b\|,\qquad m:=\sigma_{\min}(b).

By Proposition3.2, for everyε>0\varepsilon>0,mM3​𝒫SA​(εm​M)≤𝒫SB​(ε)≤Mm3​𝒫SA​(εm​M).\frac{m}{M^{3}}\,\mathcal{P}_{S_{A}}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{mM}\right)\leq\mathcal{P}_{S_{B}}(\varepsilon)\leq\frac{M}{m^{3}}\,\mathcal{P}_{S_{A}}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{mM}\right).

Chooseε0′:=m​M​ε0.\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}:=mM\,\varepsilon_{0}.

Then for0<ε<ε0′0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}^{\prime}we have0<εm​M<ε0.0<\frac{\varepsilon}{mM}<\varepsilon_{0}.

Hence the assumed bounds for𝒫SA\mathcal{P}_{S_{A}}givec1​(εm​M)−β≤𝒫SA​(εm​M)≤c2​(εm​M)−β.c_{1}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{mM}\right)^{-\beta}\leq\mathcal{P}_{S_{A}}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{mM}\right)\leq c_{2}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{mM}\right)^{-\beta}.

Substituting these inequalities into the comparison estimate yieldsmM3​c1​(m​M)β​ε−β≤𝒫SB​(ε)≤Mm3​c2​(m​M)β​ε−β.\frac{m}{M^{3}}c_{1}(mM)^{\beta}\,\varepsilon^{-\beta}\leq\mathcal{P}_{S_{B}}(\varepsilon)\leq\frac{M}{m^{3}}c_{2}(mM)^{\beta}\,\varepsilon^{-\beta}.

Thus the conclusion holds withc1′=mM3​c1​(m​M)β,c2′=Mm3​c2​(m​M)β.c_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{m}{M^{3}}c_{1}(mM)^{\beta},\qquad c_{2}^{\prime}=\frac{M}{m^{3}}c_{2}(mM)^{\beta}.

∎

## Lemma 3.4(Puiseux asymptotics implies Poisson asymptotics).

Letf≥0f\geq 0be locally integrable near0, and assume that for someα∈[0,1)\alpha\in[0,1)andδ∈(0,1+α)\delta\in(0,1+\alpha),f​(x)=c+​x−α+O​(x−α+δ)as​x↓0,f(x)=c_{+}\,x^{-\alpha}+O(x^{-\alpha+\delta})\quad\text{as }x\downarrow 0,

andf​(−x)=c−​x−α+O​(x−α+δ)as​x↓0,f(-x)=c_{-}\,x^{-\alpha}+O(x^{-\alpha+\delta})\quad\text{as }x\downarrow 0,

withc+,c−≥0c_{+},c_{-}\geq 0andc++c−>0c_{+}+c_{-}>0.
Then1π​∫ℝε​f​(x)x2+ε2​𝑑x=c++c−2​sec⁡(π​α2)​ε−α+O​(ε−α+δ).\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\varepsilon\,f(x)}{x^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}}\,dx=\frac{c_{+}+c_{-}}{2}\,\sec\!\Bigl(\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\Bigr)\,\varepsilon^{-\alpha}+O(\varepsilon^{-\alpha+\delta}).

Proof is in AppendixB.1.

## Lemma 3.5(Poisson asymptotics determines the leading Puiseux exponent).

Letffbe the density of the absolutely continuous part of a matrix semicircle.
Assume thatffhas an algebraic singularity at0and no atom at0.
Then there exist exponents−1<β1<β2<⋯<βN-1<\beta_{1}<\beta_{2}<\cdots<\beta_{N}

and coefficientsak±∈ℝa_{k}^{\pm}\in\mathbb{R}such thatf​(x)=∑k=1Nak+​xβk+o​(xβN)as​x↓0,f(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{N}a_{k}^{+}x^{\beta_{k}}+o(x^{\beta_{N}})\quad\text{as }x\downarrow 0,f​(−x)=∑k=1Nak−​xβk+o​(xβN)as​x↓0.f(-x)=\sum_{k=1}^{N}a_{k}^{-}x^{\beta_{k}}+o(x^{\beta_{N}})\quad\text{as }x\downarrow 0.

Letβ∗=min⁡{βk:ak+≠0​or​ak−≠0}\beta_{*}=\min\{\beta_{k}:\ a_{k}^{+}\neq 0\text{ or }a_{k}^{-}\neq 0\}and setα∗=−β∗∈[0,1)\alpha_{*}=-\beta_{*}\in[0,1).
Then𝒫X​(ε)≍ε−α∗(ε↓0).\mathcal{P}_{X}(\varepsilon)\asymp\varepsilon^{-\alpha_{*}}\qquad(\varepsilon\downarrow 0).

Conversely, if𝒫X​(ε)≍ε−α(ε↓0),\mathcal{P}_{X}(\varepsilon)\asymp\varepsilon^{-\alpha}\qquad(\varepsilon\downarrow 0),

thenα=α∗\alpha=\alpha_{*}.

Proof is in AppendixB.2

## Theorem 3.6(Invariance of the leading singularity exponent under covariance-map scaling).

LetSA=∑i=1rAi⊗si,SB=∑j=1qBj⊗tjS_{A}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}A_{i}\otimes s_{i},\qquad S_{B}=\sum_{j=1}^{q}B_{j}\otimes t_{j}

be matrix semicircles, with covariance mapsηA​(X)=∑i=1rAi​X​Ai∗,ηB​(X)=∑j=1qBj​X​Bj∗.\eta_{A}(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}A_{i}XA_{i}^{*},\qquad\eta_{B}(X)=\sum_{j=1}^{q}B_{j}XB_{j}^{*}.

Assume thatηA\eta_{A}andηB\eta_{B}are symmetrically scalable.

Assume moreover thatSAS_{A}is full, so that neitherSAS_{A}norSBS_{B}has an atom
at0. LetfAf_{A}andfBf_{B}denote the scalar densities ofSAS_{A}andSBS_{B}.

Suppose thatfAf_{A}has an algebraic singularity at0with leading exponent−α-\alpha, whereα∈[0,1)\alpha\in[0,1). In particular, near0,fA​(x)=cA,+​x−α+o​(x−α)(x↓0),f_{A}(x)=c_{A,+}\,x^{-\alpha}+o(x^{-\alpha})\qquad(x\downarrow 0),

andfA​(−x)=cA,−​x−α+o​(x−α)(x↓0),f_{A}(-x)=c_{A,-}\,x^{-\alpha}+o(x^{-\alpha})\qquad(x\downarrow 0),

withcA,+,cA,−≥0c_{A,+},c_{A,-}\geq 0andcA,++cA,−>0c_{A,+}+c_{A,-}>0.

ThenfBf_{B}has the same leading exponent−α-\alphaat0; that is,fB​(x)=cB,+​x−α+o​(x−α)(x↓0),f_{B}(x)=c_{B,+}\,x^{-\alpha}+o(x^{-\alpha})\qquad(x\downarrow 0),

andfB​(−x)=cB,−​x−α+o​(x−α)(x↓0),f_{B}(-x)=c_{B,-}\,x^{-\alpha}+o(x^{-\alpha})\qquad(x\downarrow 0),

for some constantscB,+,cB,−≥0c_{B,+},c_{B,-}\geq 0, not both zero.

In particular, the leading singularity exponent at0is invariant under
symmetric scalability of covariance maps.

## Proof.

By Proposition2.4, the scalar Cauchy transform ofSBS_{B}is algebraic. Hence the densityfBf_{B}has the local Puiseux-type
expansion required in Lemma3.5. SinceSAS_{A}is full
andηB\eta_{B}is obtained fromηA\eta_{A}by an invertible symmetric scaling,SBS_{B}has no atom at0.

By Lemma3.4, the assumed asymptotics offAf_{A}imply
that there existsδ>0\delta>0such that𝒫SA​(ε)=cA,++cA,−2​sec⁡(π​α2)​ε−α+O​(ε−α+δ)(ε↓0).\mathcal{P}_{S_{A}}(\varepsilon)=\frac{c_{A,+}+c_{A,-}}{2}\,\sec\!\left(\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\right)\,\varepsilon^{-\alpha}+O(\varepsilon^{-\alpha+\delta})\qquad(\varepsilon\downarrow 0).

In particular,𝒫SA​(ε)≍ε−α(ε↓0).\mathcal{P}_{S_{A}}(\varepsilon)\asymp\varepsilon^{-\alpha}\qquad(\varepsilon\downarrow 0).

Therefore, by Corollary3.3,𝒫SB​(ε)≍ε−α(ε↓0).\mathcal{P}_{S_{B}}(\varepsilon)\asymp\varepsilon^{-\alpha}\qquad(\varepsilon\downarrow 0).

Now Lemma3.5applies toSBS_{B}. Letα∗∈[0,1)\alpha_{*}\in[0,1)denote the leading singularity exponent offBf_{B}in the
sense of that lemma. Since𝒫SB​(ε)≍ε−α,\mathcal{P}_{S_{B}}(\varepsilon)\asymp\varepsilon^{-\alpha},

the converse part of Lemma3.5givesα∗=α.\alpha_{*}=\alpha.

Hence the leading term in the Puiseux expansion offBf_{B}has exponent−α-\alpha. Equivalently, there exist constantscB,+,cB,−≥0c_{B,+},c_{B,-}\geq 0, not
both zero, such thatfB​(x)=cB,+​x−α+o​(x−α)(x↓0),f_{B}(x)=c_{B,+}\,x^{-\alpha}+o(x^{-\alpha})\qquad(x\downarrow 0),

andfB​(−x)=cB,−​x−α+o​(x−α)(x↓0).f_{B}(-x)=c_{B,-}\,x^{-\alpha}+o(x^{-\alpha})\qquad(x\downarrow 0).

This proves the claim.
∎

Remark: This is Result A of the introduction.

## §4  Hermitian binary pencils and reduction to canonical cells

## §4.1  Lancaster–Rodman canonical cells

LetA=A1​x1+A2​x2A=A_{1}x_{1}+A_{2}x_{2}be the associated Hermitian matrix pencil.
According toLancaster and Rodman (2005), every Hermitian binary pencil
can be brought by a congruence transformationA↦C​A​C∗A\mapsto CAC^{*}to a direct sum of
indecomposable cells of the following three types.

Type I(eigenvalue at∞\infty):δ​(Fn​x1+Gn​x2)\delta\,(F_{n}\,x_{1}+G_{n}\,x_{2}),δ=±1\delta=\pm 1,n≥1n\geq 1.

Type II(real finite eigenvalueα\alpha):η​(Fn​(x2+α​x1)+Gn​x1)\eta\,\bigl(F_{n}\,(x_{2}+\alpha\,x_{1})+G_{n}\,x_{1}\bigr),η=±1\eta=\pm 1,α∈ℝ\alpha\in\mathbb{R},n≥1n\geq 1.

Type III(conjugate pair of non-real eigenvaluesβ,β¯\beta,\overline{\beta}):[0Fm​(x2+β​x1)+Gm​x1Fm​(x2+β¯​x1)+Gm​x10],β∈ℂ∖ℝ,m≥1.\begin{bmatrix}0&F_{m}(x_{2}+\beta\,x_{1})+G_{m}\,x_{1}\\
F_{m}(x_{2}+\overline{\beta}\,x_{1})+G_{m}\,x_{1}&0\end{bmatrix},\qquad\beta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R},\quad m\geq 1.

HereFmF_{m}is them×mm\times msip(standard involutory permutation) matrix
with ones on the anti-diagonal,Fm=[0⋯01⋮⋰00⋰⋮10⋯0]=Fm−1,F_{m}=\begin{bmatrix}0&\cdots&0&1\\
\vdots&&\iddots&0\\
0&\iddots&&\vdots\\
1&0&\cdots&0\end{bmatrix}=F_{m}^{-1},

andGm=[Fm−1000]G_{m}=\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}F_{m-1}&0\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr].
A Type I cell has sizenn, a Type II cell has sizenn, and a Type III cell has size2​m2m.
The signsδ,η=±1\delta,\eta=\pm 1constitute thesign characteristicof the pencil.
The canonical form is unique up to permutation of blocks and the replacementβ↔β¯\beta\leftrightarrow\overline{\beta}within Type III cells.

## Remark 4.1.

A1×11\times 1cell of Type I is simplyδ​x1\delta\,x_{1}and a1×11\times 1cell of
Type II isη​(x2+α​x1)\eta\,(x_{2}+\alpha\,x_{1}); both are scalar pencils giving rise to
(rescaled) scalar semicircular distributions with no singularity.
Forn≥2n\geq 2, Type I and II cells are splittable but not LR-semisimple, and the
associated matrix semicircular element develops a singularity at the origin.
A Type III cell of size22(i.e.,m=1m=1) is splittable and LR-semisimple; form≥2m\geq 2it is again splittable but not LR-semisimple.

## Definition 4.2(Regular and full pencils).

LetA=A1​x1+⋯+Ar​xrA=A_{1}x_{1}+\dots+A_{r}x_{r}be a self-adjoint matrix pencil,Ai=Ai∗∈MN​(ℂ)A_{i}=A_{i}^{*}\in M_{N}(\mathbb{C}).
- (i)

AAisregularifdet(A1​x1+⋯+Ar​xr)≢0\det(A_{1}x_{1}+\dots+A_{r}x_{r})\not\equiv 0inℂ​[x1,…,xr]\mathbb{C}[x_{1},\dots,x_{r}];
equivalently, some real combination∑iti​Ai\sum_{i}t_{i}A_{i}(t∈ℝrt\in\mathbb{R}^{r}) is invertible.
- (ii)

AAisfullif its noncommutative inner rank is maximal,rank⁡A=N\operatorname{rank}A=N, whererank⁡A\operatorname{rank}Ais the leastρ\rhoadmitting a factorizationA=P​QA=PQwithP∈MN×ρ​(ℂ​⟨x1,…,xr⟩)P\in M_{N\times\rho}(\mathbb{C}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\rangle)andQ∈Mρ×N​(ℂ​⟨x1,…,xr⟩)Q\in M_{\rho\times N}(\mathbb{C}\langle x_{1},\dots,x_{r}\rangle).

By(Hoffmannet al.,2026, Thm. 4.3), for free standard semicircular generators the
inner rank is read off the atom of the scalar law,rank⁡A=N​(1−μSA​({0}))\operatorname{rank}A=N\bigl(1-\mu_{S_{A}}(\{0\})\bigr); thusAAis full iffμSA​({0})=0\mu_{S_{A}}(\{0\})=0. This is the
sense of “full” used throughout (no atom at the origin).

## Proposition 4.3(For binary pencils, regularity==fullness).

LetA=A1​x1+A2​x2A=A_{1}x_{1}+A_{2}x_{2}be a self-adjoint binary pencil withAi∈MN​(ℂ)A_{i}\in M_{N}(\mathbb{C}). The following
are equivalent:(i)AAis regular;(ii)AAis full;(iii)μSA​({0})=0\mu_{S_{A}}(\{0\})=0.

## Proof.

The equivalence (ii)⇔\iff(iii) is(Hoffmannet al.,2026, Thm. 4.3).

(i)⇒\Rightarrow(ii), by contraposition. IfAAis not full thenrankA=:r<N\operatorname{rank}A=:r<N, soA=P​QA=PQwithP,QP,Qmatrices overℂ​⟨x1,x2⟩\mathbb{C}\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangleof inner sizerr. Specializing the formal variables to any scalars(x1,x2)=(t1,t2)∈ℂ2(x_{1},x_{2})=(t_{1},t_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}givesA1​t1+A2​t2=P​(t)​Q​(t)A_{1}t_{1}+A_{2}t_{2}=P(t)Q(t), a product factoring through anrr-dimensional space, sorank⁡(A1​t1+A2​t2)≤r<N\operatorname{rank}(A_{1}t_{1}+A_{2}t_{2})\leq r<Nanddet(A1​t1+A2​t2)=0\det(A_{1}t_{1}+A_{2}t_{2})=0. As this holds for all(t1,t2)(t_{1},t_{2}), the formdet(A1​x1+A2​x2)\det(A_{1}x_{1}+A_{2}x_{2})vanishes identically:AAis singular.

(ii)⇒\Rightarrow(i), again by contraposition. AssumeAAis singular. By
the Kronecker canonical form of a pencil under strict equivalence(Gantmacher,1959, Ch. XII), there are invertible constant matricesP,Q∈GLN​(ℂ)P,Q\in\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})such
thatP​A​QPAQis a direct sum of canonical blocks, among which —AAbeing square and
singular — there is at least onecolumnminimal-index blockLϵ=x1​[Iϵ​0]+x2​[0Iϵ](ϵ×(ϵ+1),ϵ≥0).L_{\epsilon}\;=\;x_{1}\,[\,I_{\epsilon}\ \ 0\,]\;+\;x_{2}\,[\,0\ \ I_{\epsilon}\,]\qquad(\epsilon\times(\epsilon+1),\ \epsilon\geq 0).

LetW⊆ℂNW\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{N}be the(ϵ+1)(\epsilon+1)-dimensional coordinate subspace spanned by the
columns of this block, and writeA^i:=P​Ai​Q\widehat{A}_{i}:=PA_{i}Qfor the coefficients ofP​A​QPAQ. By
block-diagonalityA^1​W+A^2​W\widehat{A}_{1}W+\widehat{A}_{2}Wlies in theϵ\epsilon-dimensional row space of the
block, sodim(A^1​W+A^2​W)≤ϵ<ϵ+1=dimW\dim(\widehat{A}_{1}W+\widehat{A}_{2}W)\leq\epsilon<\epsilon+1=\dim W. PutW′:=Q​WW^{\prime}:=QW; sincePPis
invertible,dim(A1​W′+A2​W′)=dim(A^1​W+A^2​W)<dimW=dimW′,\dim(A_{1}W^{\prime}+A_{2}W^{\prime})=\dim(\widehat{A}_{1}W+\widehat{A}_{2}W)<\dim W=\dim W^{\prime},

soW′W^{\prime}is ashrunk subspacefor the Kraus family{A1,A2}\{A_{1},A_{2}\}— equivalently the
completely positive mapη​(X)=A1​X​A1+A2​X​A2\eta(X)=A_{1}XA_{1}+A_{2}XA_{2}strictly decreases the rank of the
projection ontoW′W^{\prime}. By the operator-scaling characterization of fullness(Garget al.,2020, §1 and App.),AAis not full.
∎

## Remark 4.4(The equivalence is special tor=2r=2).

Forr≥3r\geq 3fullness is strictly weaker than regularity — this gap is exactly what makes
the noncommutative Edmonds problem nontrivialHoffmannet al.(2026); Garget al.(2020). The
standard witness is the Hermitian dressing of the generic3×33\times 3skew form,A1​x1+A2​x2+A3​x3=i​(0x1x2−x10x3−x2−x30)A_{1}x_{1}+A_{2}x_{2}+A_{3}x_{3}=i\!\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&x_{1}&x_{2}\\
-x_{1}&0&x_{3}\\
-x_{2}&-x_{3}&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)with HermitianAkA_{k}(the±i\pm ioff the diagonal): it hasdet≡0\det\equiv 0, hence is singular, yetrank=3\operatorname{rank}=3, hence full. Forr=2r=2the gap closes because
Kronecker’s form confines the singularity to a column block. Accordingly
Proposition4.3is stated for binary pencils, which is the setting of
§§5–9.

## Lemma 4.5(Types II and III preserve no maximal abelian subalgebra).

LetS=∑iAi⊗siS=\sum_{i}A_{i}\otimes s_{i}be a matrix semicircular element with Hermitian
coefficientsAi∈MN​(ℂ)A_{i}\in M_{N}(\mathbb{C})and covariance mapη​(X)=∑iAi​X​Ai\eta(X)=\sum_{i}A_{i}XA_{i}, and
writeμk:=(id⊗φ)​(Sk)\mu_{k}:=(\mathrm{id}\otimes\varphi)(S^{k})for its matrix-valued moments.
- (1)

The odd moments vanish,μ1=μ3=0\mu_{1}=\mu_{3}=0, whileμ2=η​(I)\mu_{2}=\eta(I)andμ4=η2​(I)+η​(I)2\mu_{4}=\eta^{2}(I)+\eta(I)^{2}; consequently(13)[μ2,μ4]=[η​(I),η2​(I)].[\mu_{2},\mu_{4}]=[\eta(I),\eta^{2}(I)].
- (2)

For a Type II cell(A1=α​Fn+Gn,A2=Fn,α∈ℝ∖{0},n≥2)\bigl(A_{1}=\alpha F_{n}+G_{n},\ A_{2}=F_{n},\ \alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\},\ n\geq 2\bigr)and for a Type III cell(N=2​m,m≥2,β∈ℂ∖ℝ)\bigl(N=2m,\ m\geq 2,\ \beta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}\bigr)one has[η​(I),η2​(I)]≠0[\eta(I),\eta^{2}(I)]\neq 0. Henceη\etaleaves no maximal abelian subalgebra ofMN​(ℂ)M_{N}(\mathbb{C})invariant.

Proof is in AppendixC.

## Remark 4.6(Variance-profile reading of Lemma4.5).

The scalar law ofSSis the law of a variance profile in some orthonormal basis
exactly whenη\etaleaves a maximal abelian subalgebra𝒜\mathcal{A}invariant: in
a basis diagonalizing𝒜\mathcal{A}the completely positive mapη\etarestricts to
a nonnegative profileS=(si​j)S=(s_{ij})on the diagonal, whose entries solve the
associated vector Dyson equation (cf. Remark5.2andKrüger and Renfrew (2025)). Lemma4.5shows this fails for Types II and III:
their scalar laws are variance-profile laws innobasis. In particular the
diagonal reduction that places Type I inside the Krüger–Renfrew framework
(Remark5.2) has no Type II/III analogue, which is why it is the
leading-order analysis, rather than a diagonal compression, that transfers to
these cells.

## §4.2  Direct sums and dominant singularities

## Proposition 4.7.

LetA∈Mn1​(ℂ)⊗𝒜A\in M_{n_{1}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathcal{A}andB∈Mn2​(ℂ)⊗𝒜B\in M_{n_{2}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathcal{A}be self-adjoint, and letC=A⊕B=(A00B)∈Mn1+n2​(ℂ)⊗𝒜.C=A\oplus B=\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&B\end{pmatrix}\in M_{n_{1}+n_{2}}(\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathcal{A}.

LetμA,μB,μC\mu_{A},\mu_{B},\mu_{C}be their scalar spectral measures (with respect to the normalized matrix traces). ThenμC=n1n1+n2​μA+n2n1+n2​μB.\mu_{C}=\frac{n_{1}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}\mu_{A}+\frac{n_{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}\mu_{B}.

In particular, ifμA\mu_{A}andμB\mu_{B}have densitiesfAf_{A}andfBf_{B}, thenμC\mu_{C}has densityfC​(x)=n1n1+n2​fA​(x)+n2n1+n2​fB​(x)f_{C}(x)=\frac{n_{1}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}f_{A}(x)+\frac{n_{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}f_{B}(x)

for almost everyxx.

## Proof.

Forz∈ℂ+z\in\mathbb{C}^{+},(z​I−C)−1=((z​I−A)−100(z​I−B)−1).(zI-C)^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}(zI-A)^{-1}&0\\
0&(zI-B)^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Applying the conditional expectationi​d⊗φid\otimes\varphigivesGC​(z)=GA​(z)⊕GB​(z).G_{C}(z)=G_{A}(z)\oplus G_{B}(z).

Now take the normalized trace onMn1+n2​(ℂ)M_{n_{1}+n_{2}}(\mathbb{C}):HC​(z)=trn1+n2⁡(GC​(z))=1n1+n2​(Tr⁡(GA​(z))+Tr⁡(GB​(z))).H_{C}(z)=\operatorname{tr}_{n_{1}+n_{2}}(G_{C}(z))=\frac{1}{n_{1}+n_{2}}\Big(\operatorname{Tr}(G_{A}(z))+\operatorname{Tr}(G_{B}(z))\Big).

SinceHA​(z)=trn1⁡(GA​(z))=1n1​Tr⁡(GA​(z)),HB​(z)=trn2⁡(GB​(z))=1n2​Tr⁡(GB​(z)),H_{A}(z)=\operatorname{tr}_{n_{1}}(G_{A}(z))=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\operatorname{Tr}(G_{A}(z)),\qquad H_{B}(z)=\operatorname{tr}_{n_{2}}(G_{B}(z))=\frac{1}{n_{2}}\operatorname{Tr}(G_{B}(z)),

we obtainHC​(z)=n1n1+n2​HA​(z)+n2n1+n2​HB​(z).H_{C}(z)=\frac{n_{1}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}H_{A}(z)+\frac{n_{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}H_{B}(z).

Therefore the scalar Cauchy transform ofμC\mu_{C}is the same convex combination of the scalar Cauchy transforms ofμA\mu_{A}andμB\mu_{B}, which impliesμC=n1n1+n2​μA+n2n1+n2​μB.\mu_{C}=\frac{n_{1}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}\mu_{A}+\frac{n_{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}\mu_{B}.

IfμA\mu_{A}andμB\mu_{B}are absolutely continuous with densitiesfAf_{A}andfBf_{B}, then the same is true forμC\mu_{C}, with densityfC​(x)=n1n1+n2​fA​(x)+n2n1+n2​fB​(x).f_{C}(x)=\frac{n_{1}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}f_{A}(x)+\frac{n_{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}f_{B}(x).

∎

## §4.3  Reduction of the classification problem

## Proposition 4.8.

LetA=A1​x1+A2​x2A=A_{1}x_{1}+A_{2}x_{2}be a Hermitian binary pencil whose matrix semicircular
elementSA=A1⊗s1+A2⊗s2∈Mn​(ℂ)⊗𝒜S_{A}=A_{1}\otimes s_{1}+A_{2}\otimes s_{2}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathcal{A}is full, and letC∈Mn​(ℂ)C\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})be invertible withC​A​C∗=B=⨁i=1kB(i)CAC^{*}=B=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}B^{(i)}

the Lancaster–Rodman canonical form,B(i)B^{(i)}an indecomposable cell of sizenin_{i},∑ini=n\sum_{i}n_{i}=n. For each cell letαi∈[0,1)\alpha_{i}\in[0,1)be the order of the singularity ofμSB(i)\mu_{S_{B^{(i)}}}at0, i.e. its leading Puiseux exponent is−αi-\alpha_{i}. ThenμSA\mu_{S_{A}}has no atom at0, and its leading Puiseux exponent there equals−α∗,α∗:=max1≤i≤k⁡αi.-\alpha_{*},\qquad\alpha_{*}:=\max_{1\leq i\leq k}\alpha_{i}.

Thus the singularity ofμSA\mu_{S_{A}}at0is governed by the most singular cell — the
one of largestαi\alpha_{i}, equivalently smallest (most negative) leading exponent−αi-\alpha_{i}— andαi\alpha_{i}depends only on the type and size ofB(i)B^{(i)}, not on its
sign characteristic. Consequently, classifying the singularity at0of an arbitrary
Hermitian binary pencil reduces to computingαi\alpha_{i}for each indecomposable canonical cell.

## Proof.

WriteSB=∑jBj⊗sjS_{B}=\sum_{j}B_{j}\otimes s_{j}for the matrix semicircular element ofBB; sinceB=C​A​C∗B=CAC^{*}we haveSB=(C⊗I)​SA​(C∗⊗I)S_{B}=(C\otimes I)\,S_{A}\,(C^{*}\otimes I).

behaviour at every point. The covariance map ofSpS_{p}isηSp​(X)=∑i(p​Ai​p)​X​(p​Ai​p)=p​ηA​(p​X​p)​p,\eta_{S_{p}}(X)=\sum_{i}(pA_{i}p)\,X\,(pA_{i}p)=p\,\eta_{A}(pXp)\,p,

andμSB\mu_{S_{B}}has no atom at0.

Step 2 (the direct sum is governed by its most singular cell).Iterating Proposition4.7over thekkblocks,μSB=∑i=1knin​μSB(i),nin>0.\mu_{S_{B}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{n_{i}}{n}\,\mu_{S_{B^{(i)}}},\qquad\frac{n_{i}}{n}>0.

Evaluating on{0}\{0\}gives0=μSB​({0})=∑inin​μSB(i)​({0})0=\mu_{S_{B}}(\{0\})=\sum_{i}\frac{n_{i}}{n}\,\mu_{S_{B^{(i)}}}(\{0\}), a

Part II

Singularities of canonical binary cells

## §5  Type I cells: the chain model

We start to analyze the binary matrix semicircles and we start with the cells that have Type I in Lancaster-Rodman classification:An=Fn​x1+Gn​x2A_{n}=F_{n}x_{1}+G_{n}x_{2}.

## Theorem 5.1(Type I cells — explicit density at the origin).

For the matrix semicircleSn=Fn⊗s1+Gn⊗s2S_{n}=F_{n}\otimes s_{1}+G_{n}\otimes s_{2},
the scalar densityffsatisfiesf​(x)=sin⁡(π/(n+1))π​n​|x|−(n−1)/(n+1)+o​(|x|−(n−1)/(n+1))(|x|↓0).f(x)\;=\;\frac{\sin\!\left(\pi/(n+1)\right)}{\pi n}\,|x|^{-(n-1)/(n+1)}+o\!\left(|x|^{-(n-1)/(n+1)}\right)\quad(|x|\downarrow 0).

We will give a proof of Theorem5.1in Section5.4. We start by analyzing the matrix Cauchy transform ofSnS_{n}in more detail.

## §5.1  Diagonal reduction and the chain equations

Letη\etadenote the covariance map ofSnS_{n}. Note that for a diagonal matrixD=diag⁡(d1,…,dn)D=\operatorname{diag}(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}), we haveFn​D​Fn\displaystyle F_{n}DF_{n}=diag⁡(dn,…,d1),\displaystyle=\operatorname{diag}(d_{n},\ldots,d_{1}),Gn​D​Gn\displaystyle G_{n}DG_{n}=diag⁡(dn−1,dn−2,…,d1,0).\displaystyle=\operatorname{diag}(d_{n-1},d_{n-2},\ldots,d_{1},0).

Henceη\etapreserves the sub-algebra of diagonal matrices,η​(D)=diag⁡(dn+dn−1,dn−1+dn−2,…,d2+d1,d1).\eta(D)=\operatorname{diag}(d_{n}+d_{n-1},d_{n-1}+d_{n-2},\ldots,d_{2}+d_{1},d_{1}).

Since we can start the iterative solution from the diagonal initialW0​(u)W_{0}(u), it follows that the accretive solution of Speicher’s equation (7) is diagonal.

Let the solutionW​(u)=diag⁡(w1​(u),w2​(u),…,wn​(u))W(u)=\operatorname{diag}\big(w_{1}(u),w_{2}(u),\ldots,w_{n}(u)\big). Then we have the system of equations:(wn​(u)+wn−1​(u)+u)​w1​(u)=1,\displaystyle\Big(w_{n}(u)+w_{n-1}(u)+u\Big)w_{1}(u)=1,(wn−1​(u)+wn−2​(u)+u)​w2​(u)=1,\displaystyle\Big(w_{n-1}(u)+w_{n-2}(u)+u\Big)w_{2}(u)=1,…\displaystyle\ldots(w2​(u)+w1​(u)+u)​wn−1​(u)=1,\displaystyle\Big(w_{2}(u)+w_{1}(u)+u\Big)w_{n-1}(u)=1,(w1​(u)+u)​wn​(u)=1.\displaystyle\Big(w_{1}(u)+u\Big)w_{n}(u)=1.

Lety1=wny_{1}=w_{n},y2=w1y_{2}=w_{1},y3=wn−1y_{3}=w_{n-1},y4=w2y_{4}=w_{2}, …. Formally,
sety2​m−1​(u)=wn+1−m​(u),y2​m​(u)=wm​(u),y_{2m-1}(u)=w_{n+1-m}(u),\qquad y_{2m}(u)=w_{m}(u),

whenever these indices are defined. Then(y1,y2,y3,y4,…)=(wn,w1,wn−1,w2,…),(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3},y_{4},\dots)=(w_{n},w_{1},w_{n-1},w_{2},\dots),

and the system for the functionsyk​(u)y_{k}(u)’s is(14)y1​(u+y2)\displaystyle y_{1}\big(u+y_{2}\big)=1,\displaystyle=1,…\displaystyle\ldotsyk​(u+yk−1+yk+1)\displaystyle y_{k}\big(u+y_{k-1}+y_{k+1}\big)=1,2≤k≤n−1,\displaystyle=1,\qquad 2\leq k\leq n-1,…\displaystyle\ldotsyn​(u+yn−1+yn)\displaystyle y_{n}\big(u+y_{n-1}+y_{n}\big)=1.\displaystyle=1.

Observe that the right boundary equation involvesyny_{n}quadratically, reflecting the self-coupling at the midpoint of the chain.

We are interested in the behavior of
eachyk​(u)y_{k}(u)asu↓0u\downarrow 0.

## Reduction to a two-step recurrence

Adopt the conventionsy0:=0y_{0}:=0andyn+1:=yny_{n+1}:=y_{n}. Dividing each equation
in (14) by the correspondingyky_{k}and solving for the successor
gives the uniform rewrite(15)yk+1=1yk−u−yk−1,k=1,…,n,y_{k+1}\;=\;\frac{1}{y_{k}}-u-y_{k-1},\qquad k=1,\dots,n,

subject to the boundary conditions(16)y0=0,yn+1=yn.y_{0}=0,\qquad y_{n+1}=y_{n}.

Indeed, fork=1k=1(15) reduces toy2=1/y1−uy_{2}=1/y_{1}-u, which is the
first equation of (14); for2≤k≤n−12\leq k\leq n-1it is the generic
equation; and fork=nk=n, the conditionyn+1=yny_{n+1}=y_{n}is exactlyyn=1/yn−u−yn−1y_{n}=1/y_{n}-u-y_{n-1}, i.e. the last equation of (14).

Equation (15) is the autonomous discrete Painlevé I map (also
known as the McMillan map),T:(x,y)⟼(y,1y−u−x),T:(x,y)\longmapsto\bigl(y,\,\tfrac{1}{y}-u-x\bigr),

withuuplaying the role of a parameter. The system (14) is thus
the orbit ofTTstarting from(y0,y1)=(0,y1)(y_{0},y_{1})=(0,y_{1})and terminating on the
diagonal{yn+1=yn}\{y_{n+1}=y_{n}\}.

## Remark 5.2(Type I as a variance profile).

The diagonal reduction places Type I inside the variance-profile framework ofKrüger and Renfrew (2025). The compression ofη\etato the diagonal
subalgebra is the symmetric nonnegative profileS=(si​j)S=(s_{ij})withsi​j=|(Fn)i​j|2+|(Gn)i​j|2=𝟏​[i+j=n+1]+𝟏​[i+j=n]s_{ij}=|(F_{n})_{ij}|^{2}+|(G_{n})_{ij}|^{2}=\mathbf{1}[i+j=n+1]+\mathbf{1}[i+j=n],
supported on two adjacent antidiagonals, and the diagonal entries ofGSG_{S}solve
the associated vector Dyson equation. ThisSShas support but not total support, so by(Krüger and Renfrew,2025, Prop. 2.1)the scalar density blows up at the origin, and by(Krüger and Renfrew,2025, Thm. 2.8)thesingularity degreeequalsσ=ℓ/(ℓ+2)\sigma=\ell/(\ell+2), withℓ\ellthe
longest increasing chain in the zero pattern ofSS; hereℓ=n−1\ell=n-1, givingσ=(n−1)/(n+1)\sigma=(n-1)/(n+1)as in Theorem5.1. Thus the exponent
is not new, and the convergent fractional-power expansion of theyky_{k}may be
taken from(Krüger and Renfrew,2025, Prop. 4.1).
The same exponent was obtained independently and contemporaneously byKolupaiev (2021), whose Assumption 1.1 (si​j>0s_{ij}>0fori+j∈{n,n+1}i+j\in\{n,n+1\}andsi​j=0s_{ij}=0fori+j≥n+2i+j\geq n+2) is exactly this critical
antidiagonal staircase: by a direct asymptotic argument he derivesρ​(E)∼|E|−(n−1)/(n+1)\rho(E)\sim|E|^{-(n-1)/(n+1)}together with the componentwise asymptoticsmk​(z)​z−(1−2​kn+1)→ck​ei​π​k/(n+1)m_{k}(z)\,z^{-(1-\frac{2k}{n+1})}\to c_{k}\,e^{\,i\pi k/(n+1)}. As inKrüger and Renfrew (2025),
the leading amplitudesck>0c_{k}>0there are pinned only implicitly, as the unique
solution of a log-linear system, so neither reference supplies the sharp constant
of Theorem5.1.

What the chain (14) adds is
its integrable structure—it is an orbit of the autonomous discrete
Painlevé I (McMillan) map (§5.2)—which we use to
obtain thesharp leading constantin closed form: the proof of(Krüger and Renfrew,2025, Thm. 2.8)reduces this constant to a positive amplitude that it
leaves undetermined, and the McMillan structure evaluates that amplitude, giving
the value in Theorem5.1.
The reduction itself is special to Type I: Types II and III preserve no maximal
abelian subalgebra, and it is the leading-order method, not this remark, that
transfers to them.

## §5.2  Integral of motion and endpoint identity

## Proposition 5.3(QRT/McMillan invariant).

The functionH​(x,y)=(x​y−1)​(x+u)​(y+u)+u2H(x,y)\;=\;(xy-1)(x+u)(y+u)+u^{2}

is invariant underTT:H​(T​(x,y))=H​(x,y)H(T(x,y))=H(x,y).

## Proof.

WriteT​(x,y)=(y,z)T(x,y)=(y,z),z=1/y−u−xz=1/y-u-x. The defining relationy​(u+x+z)=1y(u+x+z)=1givesy​z−1=−y​(x+u)yz-1=-y(x+u)andy​(z+u)=1−x​y=−(x​y−1)y(z+u)=1-xy=-(xy-1). Hence(y​z−1)​(y+u)​(z+u)\displaystyle(yz-1)(y+u)(z+u)=[−y​(x+u)]​(y+u)​(z+u)\displaystyle=\bigl[-y(x+u)\bigr](y+u)(z+u)=−(x+u)​(y+u)⋅y​(z+u)=(x+u)​(y+u)​(x​y−1),\displaystyle=-(x+u)(y+u)\cdot y(z+u)=(x+u)(y+u)(xy-1),

which is symmetric in the roles of the two pairs, soH​(y,z)=H​(x,y)H(y,z)=H(x,y).
∎

The value ofHHon our orbit is pinned down by the Dirichlet boundary
condition:H​(y0,y1)=H​(0,y1)=(−1)⋅u⋅(y1+u)+u2=−u​y1.H(y_{0},y_{1})\;=\;H(0,y_{1})\;=\;(-1)\cdot u\cdot(y_{1}+u)+u^{2}\;=\;-u\,y_{1}.

Consequently,(17)(yk−1​yk−1)​(yk−1+u)​(yk+u)=−u​(y1+u),k=1,…,n.(y_{k-1}y_{k}-1)(y_{k-1}+u)(y_{k}+u)\;=\;-u(y_{1}+u),\qquad k=1,\dots,n.

## The endpoint identity

## Proposition 5.4(Endpoint identity).

Any solution(y1,…,yn)(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})of (14) satisfies(18)(yn2−1)​(yn+u)2=−u​(y1+u).(y_{n}^{2}-1)(y_{n}+u)^{2}\;=\;-u(y_{1}+u).

## Proof.

Apply Proposition5.3to the pair(yn,yn+1)=(yn,yn)(y_{n},y_{n+1})=(y_{n},y_{n}).
SinceHHis constant along the orbit,H​(yn,yn)=H​(y0,y1)=−u​y1,H(y_{n},y_{n})\;=\;H(y_{0},y_{1})\;=\;-u\,y_{1},

which expands to(yn2−1)​(yn+u)2+u2=−u​y1(y_{n}^{2}-1)(y_{n}+u)^{2}+u^{2}=-uy_{1}, i.e. (18).
∎

Equation (18) relates the two extreme entries of the chain by a single
polynomial identity, independent ofnn. Together with
(17) it provides a very efficient replacement for
iterating (15): every consecutive pair satisfies the same
algebraic equation, whose right-hand side tends to0asu↓0u\downarrow 0.

## §5.3  Puiseux exponents

Assume eachyk​(u)y_{k}(u)admits a leading Puiseux behavior(19)yk​(u)=ck​uβk​(1+o​(1)),u↓0,y_{k}(u)\;=\;c_{k}\,u^{\beta_{k}}\bigl(1+o(1)\bigr),\qquad u\downarrow 0,

Then the exponents are uniquely determined, and(20)βk=(−1)kn+1−2​⌈k/2⌉n+1,k=1,…,n.\boxed{\;\beta_{k}\;=\;(-1)^{k}\,\frac{n+1-2\lceil k/2\rceil}{n+1},\qquad k=1,\dots,n.\;}

Equivalently, writingk=2​m−1k=2m-1andk=2​mk=2m,β2​m−1=−n−2​m+1n+1,β2​m=n−2​m+1n+1.\beta_{2m-1}\;=\;-\frac{\,n-2m+1\,}{n+1},\qquad\beta_{2m}\;=\;\phantom{-}\frac{\,n-2m+1\,}{n+1}.

## Proof of Theorem5.5.

All asymptotic relations are asu↓0u\downarrow 0.
The proof uses two tools in alternation: the orbit

σk=0\sigma_{k}=0: thenyk−1​yk→ck−1​cky_{k-1}y_{k}\to c_{k-1}c_{k},

case never occurs.

for all admissiblemm. The inductive step has two halves:

both differences(31)β2​m−(−β2​m+1)\displaystyle\beta_{2m}-(-\beta_{2m+1})=β2​m+β2​m+1=1+β1>0,\displaystyle=\beta_{2m}+\beta_{2m+1}=1+\beta_{1}>0,(32)1−(−β2​m+1)\displaystyle 1-(-\beta_{2m+1})=1+β2​m+1=2+β1+β2​m−1>0,\displaystyle=1+\beta_{2m+1}=2+\beta_{1}+\beta_{2m-1}>0,

We now assemble the proof of the sharp density theorem
(Theorem5.1). Two ingredients are

ATauberian transfer(Corollary5.7) that converts the Puiseux

density.
- (ii)

AStieltjes inversionthat reads off the sharp
leading constant of the scalar densityf​(x)f(x)from the trace of
the matrix Cauchy transform.

Theorem5.5:

## Corollary 5.7(Spectral regularity along the chain).

Under the Puiseux hypothesis of Theorem5.5, the diagonal
spectral densities at the origin behave asρk​(x)∼c~k​|x|βk,βk=(−1)k​n+1−2​⌈k/2⌉n+1.\rho_{k}(x)\;\sim\;\tilde{c}_{k}\,|x|^{\beta_{k}},\qquad\beta_{k}=(-1)^{k}\,\frac{n+1-2\lceil k/2\rceil}{n+1}.

In particular:
- •

the odd-indexed entriesρ2​m−1\rho_{2m-1}have integrable power-law
blow-ups of order(n+1−2​m)/(n+1)(n+1-2m)/(n+1);
- •

the even-indexed entriesρ2​m\rho_{2m}have power-law cusps of the
same order(n+1−2​m)/(n+1)(n+1-2m)/(n+1);

fornnodd, the terminal entryρn\rho_{n}is regular
at0(βn=0\beta_{n}=0); fornneven, all diagonal entries are non-regular.

## §6  Type II cells: statement and regularization strategy

The second case that we consider isAn=Fn​x1+(α​Fn+Gn)​x2A_{n}=F_{n}x_{1}+(\alpha F_{n}+G_{n})x_{2}, whereα≠0\alpha\neq 0is a real parameter.

## Theorem 6.1(Type II cells — explicit density at the origin).

For the matrix semicircleSn=Fn⊗s1+(α​Fn+Gn)⊗s2S_{n}=F_{n}\otimes s_{1}+(\alpha F_{n}+G_{n})\otimes s_{2}, with a realα≠0\alpha\neq 0,
the scalar densityfαf_{\alpha}satisfiesfα​(x)=Cα,n​|x|−n−1n+1+o​(|x|−n−1n+1),x→0,f_{\alpha}(x)=C_{\alpha,n}\,|x|^{-\frac{n-1}{n+1}}+o\left(|x|^{-\frac{n-1}{n+1}}\right),\qquad x\to 0,

whereCα,n=1n​πsin(πn+1)(1+α2)−2​n−1n+1.\boxed{C_{\alpha,n}=\frac{1}{n\pi}\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{n+1}\right)(1+\alpha^{2})^{-\frac{2n-1}{n+1}}.}

In particular, the singularity exponent is independent ofα\alpha:fα(x)≍|x|−n−1n+1nearx=0.\boxed{f_{\alpha}(x)\asymp|x|^{-\frac{n-1}{n+1}}\quad\text{near }x=0.}

## Strategy of the proof

Theorem6.1is proved by extracting the leading behaviour oftrn⁡Wα​(u)\operatorname{tr}_{n}W_{\alpha}(u)asu↓0u\downarrow 0, whereWα​(u)=i​Gα​(i​u)W_{\alpha}(u)=iG_{\alpha}(iu)solves
Speicher’s equation; a Stieltjes inversion (§7.8) then converts the
leadingu−pu^{-p}term of the trace, withp=n−1n+1p=\tfrac{n-1}{n+1}, into the leading|x|−p|x|^{-p}term
offαf_{\alpha}and its sharp constant. Unlike the Type I cell, hereηα\eta_{\alpha}does not
preserve the diagonal, so there is no scalar chain and Speicher’s equation must be analysed
directly. The obstacle is thatWα​(u)W_{\alpha}(u)degenerates asu→0u\to 0, its entries scaling at the
distinct ratesβi=n−2​i+1n+1\beta_{i}=\tfrac{n-2i+1}{n+1}. The argument has two stages.

Stage 1 (this section): regularization and the obstruction.After recording the joint analyticity ofWαW_{\alpha}in(α,u)(\alpha,u)(§6.1) and aℤ/2\mathbb{Z}/2-symmetry that makes every spectral
invariant even inα\alpha, so that we may assumeα≥0\alpha\geq 0(§6.2), we
remove the degeneration by the rescalingMα​(t)=D​(t)−1​Wα​(u)​D​(t)−1M_{\alpha}(t)=D(t)^{-1}W_{\alpha}(u)D(t)^{-1}

withD​(t)=diag⁡(tγi)D(t)=\operatorname{diag}(t^{\gamma_{i}}),t=u1/(n+1)t=u^{1/(n+1)}(§6.3). The rescaled equation is
regular att=0t=0, with limiting equationN−1=Θ​(N)N^{-1}=\Theta(N),Θ=Ad⁡Fn\Theta=\operatorname{Ad}F_{n}.
Crucially, its positive-solution set is not a point but a manifoldℳ\mathcal{M}of dimension⌊n2/4⌋\lfloor n^{2}/4\rfloor: the linearization at the base point has kernel theΘ\Theta-odd subspaceE−E_{-}(§6.4). This kernel is the obstruction to a direct
implicit-function argument and shapes the rest of the proof.

Stage 2 (§7): Lyapunov–Schmidt and the shifted base point.SplittingN=eY+ZN=e^{Y}+ZalongE−⊕E+E_{-}\oplus E_{+}, the transverse componentZZis eliminated by the
implicit function theorem (§7.1), and the reduced equation on the kernel
variableYYis shown to be divisible byt2t^{2}(§7.2), yielding a
regular doubly-reduced equationℜ​(Y,α,t)\mathfrak{R}(Y,\alpha,t). The subtle point is that the true
solution doesnotlimit toY=0Y=0: the constant termℜ​(0,α,0)\mathfrak{R}(0,\alpha,0)is nonzero
(§7.3). We therefore compute the reduced linearization
(§7.4), locate the correct base pointY∗​(α)∈E−Y_{*}(\alpha)\in E_{-}in closed form
(§7.5), and recenter there (§7.6); atY∗Y_{*}the
reduced linearization is invertible, so the implicit function theorem produces a holomorphic
solutionMα​(t)=Mα(0)+O​(t)M_{\alpha}(t)=M_{\alpha}^{(0)}+O(t)with explicit diagonal limitMα(0)M_{\alpha}^{(0)}(§7.7). Its most singular entry gives the leading term oftrn⁡Wα​(u)\operatorname{tr}_{n}W_{\alpha}(u)and completes the proof (§7.8).

## §6.1  Joint analyticity ofWα​(u)W_{\alpha}(u)in(α,u)(\alpha,u)

The solutionWα​(u)W_{\alpha}(u)exists as a unique positive definite real symmetric matrix for everyα∈ℝ\alpha\in\mathbb{R}andu>0u>0, by the existence and uniqueness theorem ofHeltonet al.(2007). The following theorem records its smooth dependence on(α,u)(\alpha,u).

## Theorem 6.2(Joint real-analyticity in(α,u)(\alpha,u)).

The map(α,u)⟼Wα​(u)∈Symn++(\alpha,u)\;\longmapsto\;W_{\alpha}(u)\;\in\;\mathrm{Sym}_{n}^{++}

is real-analytic onℝ×(0,∞)\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty).

Proof is in AppendixD.1.

## §6.2  Aℤ/2\mathbb{Z}/2-symmetry of the familyηα\eta_{\alpha}

In this section we identify a discrete symmetry of the family of covariance maps{ηα}α∈ℝ\{\eta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}that links the values atα\alphaand−α-\alphavia a fixed congruence. The symmetry transfers to the solutions of Speicher’s equation and yields several immediate consequences. In particular, all spectral invariants ofWα​(u)W_{\alpha}(u)are even functions ofα\alpha.

## Setup and statement

Define(36)Jn:=diag⁡((−1)1,(−1)2,…,(−1)n)=diag⁡(−1,1,−1,…).J_{n}\;:=\;\operatorname{diag}\bigl((-1)^{1},(-1)^{2},\ldots,(-1)^{n}\bigr)\;=\;\operatorname{diag}(-1,1,-1,\ldots).

Note thatJn∗=JnJ_{n}^{*}=J_{n},Jn2=IJ_{n}^{2}=I, soJnJ_{n}is a real orthogonal involution.

## Proposition 6.3(ℤ/2\mathbb{Z}/2-symmetry).

For everyn≥2n\geq 2and everyα∈ℝ\alpha\in\mathbb{R},(37)Jn​ηα​(B)​Jn=η−α​(Jn​B​Jn)for all​B∈Mn​(ℂ).J_{n}\,\eta_{\alpha}(B)\,J_{n}\;=\;\eta_{-\alpha}(J_{n}BJ_{n})\qquad\text{for all }B\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C}).

Consequently, the unique accretive solutionWα​(u)∈Symn++W_{\alpha}(u)\in\mathrm{Sym}_{n}^{++}of Speicher’s equation satisfies(38)W−α​(u)=Jn​Wα​(u)​Jnfor all​α∈ℝ,u>0.W_{-\alpha}(u)\;=\;J_{n}\,W_{\alpha}(u)\,J_{n}\qquad\text{for all }\alpha\in\mathbb{R},\ u>0.

using (39) and((−1)n+1)2=1\bigl((-1)^{n+1}\bigr)^{2}=1in the first summand.

Step 3: symmetry of the solution.Conjugating Speicher’s equationWα​(u)−1=u​I+ηα​(Wα​(u))W_{\alpha}(u)^{-1}=uI+\eta_{\alpha}(W_{\alpha}(u))byJnJ_{n}and usingJn2=IJ_{n}^{2}=I:(Jn​Wα​(u)​Jn)−1\displaystyle(J_{n}W_{\alpha}(u)J_{n})^{-1}=Jn​Wα​(u)−1​Jn=u​I+Jn​ηα​(Wα​(u))​Jn\displaystyle=\;J_{n}W_{\alpha}(u)^{-1}J_{n}\;=\;uI+J_{n}\,\eta_{\alpha}(W_{\alpha}(u))\,J_{n}=(37)u​I+η−α​(Jn​Wα​(u)​Jn).\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:eta-symmetry}}}{{=}}\;uI+\eta_{-\alpha}(J_{n}W_{\alpha}(u)J_{n}).

## Remark 6.4(Even dependence on the coupling parameter).

SinceW−α​(u)=Jn​Wα​(u)​JnW_{-\alpha}(u)=J_{n}W_{\alpha}(u)J_{n}withJnJ_{n}orthogonal (Proposition6.3), every spectral
invariant ofWα​(u)W_{\alpha}(u)— eigenvalues, trace, determinant,
operator norm — is an even function ofα\alpha. Combined
with the real-analyticity ofα↦Wα​(u)\alpha\mapsto W_{\alpha}(u)(Theorem6.2), these invariants are
in fact real-analytic functions ofα2\alpha^{2}. In particular, we
may assumeα≥0\alpha\geq 0without loss of generality throughout
the remainder of this section.

indexi+ji+j.

## §6.4  Linearization and its kernel

We linearize the rescaled equation at the scalar base point and show that
the kernel of the linearization is precisely the antiinvariant subspace of
an involutionΘ\ThetaonSymn\mathrm{Sym}_{n}. The chain-order presentation
makes this kernel transparent and is the description used in §7.

## Lemma 6.6(Linearization at the scalar base point).

The operatorL0:Symn→SymnL_{0}\colon\mathrm{Sym}_{n}\to\mathrm{Sym}_{n}defined by

In particular,ker⁡L0=Symn−,im⁡L0=Symn+,\ker L_{0}=\mathrm{Sym}_{n}^{-},\qquad\operatorname{im}L_{0}=\mathrm{Sym}_{n}^{+},

with the orthogonal decompositionSymn=ker⁡L0⊕im⁡L0\mathrm{Sym}_{n}=\ker L_{0}\oplus\operatorname{im}L_{0}.

## §7  The Lyapunov–Schmidt analysis for Type II cells

We resume the analysis of §6; recall the two-stage strategy described there.
Our goal in this section is to prove the following theorem.

## Theorem 7.1(Allα\alpharescaled holomorphicity).

For every realα\alpha, the rescaled solutionMα​(t)M_{\alpha}(t)extends
holomorphically to a neighborhood oft=0t=0, andMα​(t)=Mα(0)+O​(t),M_{\alpha}(t)=M_{\alpha}^{(0)}+O(t),

whereMα(0)=diag⁡(c1−3n+1,c1−6n+1,…,c1−3​nn+1),c=1+α2.M_{\alpha}^{(0)}=\operatorname{diag}\left(c^{1-\frac{3}{n+1}},c^{1-\frac{6}{n+1}},\ldots,c^{1-\frac{3n}{n+1}}\right),\qquad c=1+\alpha^{2}.

## §7.1  The reduction

We use the kernel description of §6.4to set up a
Lyapunov–Schmidt reduction of the normalized rescaled equation
(48). The kernel of the linearization at the scalar base
point isnotan obstruction: it is the tangent space to the
manifold of limiting positive solutions, and the reduction simply
parametrizes that manifold inΘ\Theta-odd coordinates.

Decomposition.LetΘ=Ad​J\Theta=\mathrm{Ad}\,Jbe the involution onSymn\mathrm{Sym}_{n}from
§6.4. Set(53)E−:={Y∈Symn:Θ​(Y)=−Y},E+:={Z∈Symn:Θ​(Z)=Z},E_{-}:=\{Y\in\mathrm{Sym}_{n}:\Theta(Y)=-Y\},\qquad E_{+}:=\{Z\in\mathrm{Sym}_{n}:\Theta(Z)=Z\},

projections ontoE±E_{\pm}. By Lemma6.6,ker⁡L0=E−,im⁡L0=E+,L0|E+=−2​IdE+.\ker L_{0}=E_{-},\qquad\operatorname{im}L_{0}=E_{+},\qquad L_{0}|_{E_{+}}=-2\,\mathrm{Id}_{E_{+}}.

positive solutionsℳ:={N∈Symn++:N−1=Θ​(N)}.\mathcal{M}:=\{N\in\mathrm{Sym}_{n}^{++}:N^{-1}=\Theta(N)\}.

NearN∗=IN_{*}=I, the manifoldℳ\mathcal{M}is parametrized byE−E_{-}via the

Indeed, forY∈E−Y\in E_{-}one hasΘ​(Y)=−Y\Theta(Y)=-Y, henceΘ​(eY)=eΘ​(Y)=e−Y=(eY)−1,\Theta(e^{Y})=e^{\Theta(Y)}=e^{-Y}=(e^{Y})^{-1},

Splitting𝒢~\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}by the projectionsP±P_{\pm}gives theeven equationℰ​(Y,Z,α,t):=P+​𝒢~​(eY+Z,α,t)=0,\mathcal{E}(Y,Z,\alpha,t):=P_{+}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(e^{Y}+Z,\alpha,t)=0,

and theodd equationP−​𝒢~​(eY+Z,α,t)=0P_{-}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(e^{Y}+Z,\alpha,t)=0.
The even equation is transverse toℳ\mathcal{M}and is solved forZZby

problem on the kernel variableYY.

## Lemma 7.2(Solving the even equation).

There exist neighborhoodsU⊂E−U\subset E_{-}of0,V⊂ℝV\subset\mathbb{R}of any

such function. MoreoverΦ​(Y,α,0)≡0.\Phi(Y,\alpha,0)\equiv 0.

the limiting equation, so𝒢~​(I,α,0)=0\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(I,\alpha,0)=0and a

att=0t=0, we haveDZ​ℰ​(0,0,α,0)​[Z]=P+​L0​[Z]D_{Z}\mathcal{E}(0,0,\alpha,0)[Z]=P_{+}\,L_{0}[Z]. By

which is invertible. The implicit function theorem produces the uniqueΦ\PhiwithΦ​(0,α,0)=0\Phi(0,\alpha,0)=0.

One can show that the “naive” first-order linearizationA1:=P−​𝒫|E−A_{1}:=P_{-}\mathcal{P}|_{E_{-}}equals−12​{Tn,⋅}-\tfrac{1}{2}\{T_{n},\cdot\}, with

## Proposition 7.6.

For everyY∈E−Y\in E_{-}and everyα\alpha,ℛ^​(Y,α,0)=0\widehat{\mathscr{R}}(Y,\alpha,0)=0. Equivalently,ℛ\mathscr{R}is

## Proof.

andℛ^​(Y,α,0)=limt→0ℛ​(Y,α,t)t=∂tℛ​(Y,α,0).\widehat{\mathscr{R}}(Y,\alpha,0)\;=\;\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\mathscr{R}(Y,\alpha,t)}{t}\;=\;\partial_{t}\mathscr{R}(Y,\alpha,0).

brackets gives, exactly as in the proof of

usingΦ​(Y,α,0)=0\Phi(Y,\alpha,0)=0andφ​(Y):=∂tΦ​(Y,α,0)\varphi(Y):=\partial_{t}\Phi(Y,\alpha,0).
The two pieces, computed in the proof of Lemma7.5, areDN​𝒢~​(eY,α,0)​[φ​(Y)]=−A​φ​(Y)​A−Θ​(φ​(Y)),∂t𝒢~​(eY,α,0)=−a​𝒫​(eY).D_{N}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(e^{Y},\alpha,0)\bigl[\varphi(Y)\bigr]\;=\;-A\,\varphi(Y)\,A-\Theta\bigl(\varphi(Y)\bigr),\qquad\partial_{t}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}(e^{Y},\alpha,0)\;=\;-a\,\mathcal{P}(e^{Y}).

Lemma7.5), this becomesℛ^​(Y,α,0)=P−​[−A​φ​(Y)​A−φ​(Y)−a​(A​Ln+Un​A)].\widehat{\mathscr{R}}(Y,\alpha,0)\;=\;P_{-}\bigl[\,-A\,\varphi(Y)\,A\;-\;\varphi(Y)\;-\;a(AL_{n}+U_{n}A)\,\bigr].

where we used−a​𝒫​(z1)=a22​𝒫​(Tn)-a\mathcal{P}(z_{1})=\frac{a^{2}}{2}\mathcal{P}(T_{n})from (62).

hence(64)ℜ(0,α,0)=12(c−1/2−c−2)(E11−En​n),c=1+α2.\boxed{\;\;\mathfrak{R}(0,\alpha,0)\;=\;\frac{1}{2}\bigl(c^{-1/2}-c^{-2}\bigr)\,(E_{11}-E_{nn}),\qquad c=1+\alpha^{2}.\;\;}

In particularℜ​(0,0,0)=0\mathfrak{R}(0,0,0)=0, butℜ​(0,α,0)≠0\mathfrak{R}(0,\alpha,0)\neq 0forα≠0\alpha\neq 0. The implication is geometric: forα≠0\alpha\neq 0theα\alpha-perturbed solution does not converge toN=IN=Iast↓0t\downarrow 0,
but to a shifted base point inside the limiting manifoldℳ\mathcal{M}.

## Proposition 7.7(Linearization of the second reduced equation).

LetLn:=Fn​Gn,Un:=Gn​Fn,Tn:=Ln+Un.L_{n}:=F_{n}G_{n},\qquad U_{n}:=G_{n}F_{n},\qquad T_{n}:=L_{n}+U_{n}.

Then, forH∈E−H\in E_{-},(65)ℬα(H)=14​c2[2(LnHUn+UnHLn)−D∂H−HD∂]−14​c(E∂H+HE∂).\boxed{\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}(H)=\frac{1}{4c^{2}}\left[2(L_{n}HU_{n}+U_{n}HL_{n})-D_{\partial}H-HD_{\partial}\right]-\frac{1}{4\sqrt{c}}\left(E_{\partial}H+HE_{\partial}\right).}

## Remark 7.8.

The point of the proposition is that the first divided reduced equation has
zeroYY-linearization, but after the second division the linearization is
the strictly negative operatorℬα\mathcal{B}_{\alpha}. Therefore the
implicit-function theorem should be applied toℜ​(Y,α,t)=0,\mathfrak{R}(Y,\alpha,t)=0,

not to the first divided equation.

## Remark 7.12(The unperturbed case).

Atα=0\alpha=0,ℜ​(0,0,0)=0\mathfrak{R}(0,0,0)=0andℬ0\mathcal{B}_{0}is invertible, so the implicit

which we now identify.

## Lemma 7.18(Recentred invertibility).

After applying the Lyapunov–Schmidt reduction to the recentred equation in
the variableN^\widehat{N}, letℜ^​(Y^,α,t)=0\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}(\widehat{Y},\alpha,t)=0

be the corresponding second divided reduced equation. ThenDY^​ℜ^​(0,α,0):E−→E−D_{\widehat{Y}}\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}(0,\alpha,0)\colon E_{-}\to E_{-}

is invertible for every realα\alpha.

## Proof.

Letℬ^α:=DY^​ℜ^​(0,α,0).\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}:=D_{\widehat{Y}}\widehat{\mathfrak{R}}(0,\alpha,0).

We will compute the closed form ofℬ^α\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}.

The order-t2t^{2}part ofd​𝒰~​(t)d\,\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}(t)in the recentered equation (75) isd^​t2​En​n,d^:=d​νn.\widehat{d}\,t^{2}E_{nn},\qquad\widehat{d}:=d\nu_{n}.

Thus, for the computation of the second divided linearization, the relevant
truncated equation is𝒢^​(N^,α,t)=N^−1−Θ​(N^)−a^​t​𝒫​(N^)−b^​t2​𝒬​(N^)−d^​t2​En​n+O​(t3).\widehat{\mathcal{G}}(\widehat{N},\alpha,t)=\widehat{N}^{-1}-\Theta(\widehat{N})-\widehat{a}\,t\,\mathcal{P}(\widehat{N})-\widehat{b}\,t^{2}\,\mathcal{Q}(\widehat{N})-\widehat{d}\,t^{2}E_{nn}+O(t^{3}).

The recentered equation has the same algebraic form as the original
normalized equation, so the computation of
Proposition7.7applies with(a,b,d)(a,b,d)replaced by(a^,b^,d^)(\hat{a},\hat{b},\hat{d}). The resulting
linearization isℬ^α(H)=b^−a^24[2(LnHUn+UnHLn)−D∂H−HD∂]−d^4(E∂H+HE∂).\boxed{\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}(H)=\frac{\hat{b}-\hat{a}^{\,2}}{4}\bigl[2(L_{n}HU_{n}+U_{n}HL_{n})-D_{\partial}H-HD_{\partial}\bigr]-\frac{\hat{d}}{4}(E_{\partial}H+HE_{\partial}).}

HereE∂:=E11+En​n,D∂:=Ln​Un+Un​Ln.E_{\partial}:=E_{11}+E_{nn},\qquad D_{\partial}:=L_{n}U_{n}+U_{n}L_{n}.

Now we compute the two scalar coefficients. Sincea^=a​w,b^=b​w2,\widehat{a}=aw,\qquad\widehat{b}=bw^{2},

we haveb^−a^2=w2​(b−a2).\widehat{b}-\widehat{a}^{2}=w^{2}(b-a^{2}).

Butb−a2=1c−α2c2=c−α2c2=1c2.b-a^{2}=\frac{1}{c}-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{c^{2}}=\frac{c-\alpha^{2}}{c^{2}}=\frac{1}{c^{2}}.

Thereforeb^−a^2=w2c2.\widehat{b}-\widehat{a}^{2}=\frac{w^{2}}{c^{2}}.

Usingw=c3/(2​(n+1)),w=c^{3/(2(n+1))},

we getb^−a^2=c3/(n+1)−2=c−(2​n−1)/(n+1).\widehat{b}-\widehat{a}^{2}=c^{3/(n+1)-2}=c^{-(2n-1)/(n+1)}.

On the other hand,d^=d​νn.\widehat{d}=d\nu_{n}.

Sinced=c−1/2,νn=c−3​(n−1)/(2​(n+1)),d=c^{-1/2},\qquad\nu_{n}=c^{-3(n-1)/(2(n+1))},

we obtaind^=c−1/2​c−3​(n−1)/(2​(n+1))=c−(2​n−1)/(n+1).\widehat{d}=c^{-1/2}c^{-3(n-1)/(2(n+1))}=c^{-(2n-1)/(n+1)}.

Thusb^−a^2=d^>0.\boxed{\widehat{b}-\widehat{a}^{2}=\widehat{d}>0.}

Sinceb^−a^2=d^>0\hat{b}-\hat{a}^{\,2}=\hat{d}>0, the two scalar
coefficients inℬ^α\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}are both strictly
positive. The energy identity from
Step 3 of the proof of
Proposition7.7therefore gives⟨H,ℬ^α​(H)⟩HS<0\langle H,\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}(H)\rangle_{\mathrm{HS}}<0for every nonzeroH∈E−H\in E_{-}, by the same commutant
argument. Henceℬ^α\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\alpha}is strictly
negative definite and invertible.

∎
