then∫ℝuu2+x2​fα​(x)​𝑑x∼Cα,n​u−p​∫ℝ|y|−p1+y2​𝑑y.\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{u}{u^{2}+x^{2}}f_{\alpha}(x)\,dx\sim C_{\alpha,n}u^{-p}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{|y|^{-p}}{1+y^{2}}\,dy.

## §8  Type III cells: gauge reduction and spectral coincidence

Type III cells are where the spectral classification is strictly coarser than the
algebraic one, and they supply two facts used elsewhere.(i)The complex argument ofβ\betais spectrally invisible: a unitary gauge
removes the phase, so the scalar density depends only on|β||\beta|and in fact
coincides with that of a Type II cell of sizemm(half the size) with real
parameter|β||\beta|(Theorem8.3). The gauge is a
congruence, so this is consistent with the congruence-invariance of the singular
exponent (Result A).(ii)NonethelessηIII\eta_{\mathrm{III}}isnotsymmetrically equivalent
to that Type II map, nor to any direct sum of Type II maps: a congruence-invariant
of the Hermitian Kraus plane separates them
(Proposition8.9). Together these give the sharp form
of Result C.

Throughout,F:=Fm=∑i=1mEi,m+1−iF:=F_{m}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}E_{i,m+1-i}is the reversal andG:=Gm=∑i=2mEi,m+2−iG:=G_{m}=\sum_{i=2}^{m}E_{i,m+2-i}the shifted reversal; both are real symmetric.

## Remark 8.1(Convention forGmG_{m}).

The shifted reversal used here,Gm=∑i=2mEi,m+2−iG_{m}=\sum_{i=2}^{m}E_{i,m+2-i}, is supported on the bandi+j=m+2i+j=m+2, whereas the Lancaster–Rodman cells of §4.1(and the
Type I/II analyses that follow) useGmLR=[Fm−1000]=∑i=1m−1Ei,m−iG_{m}^{\mathrm{LR}}=\bigl[\begin{smallmatrix}F_{m-1}&0\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr]=\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}E_{i,m-i}, supported oni+j=mi+j=m. A direct check givesGm=Fm​GmLR​FmG_{m}=F_{m}G_{m}^{\mathrm{LR}}F_{m}, so conjugation by the real orthogonal involutionFmF_{m}(applied blockwise, i.e. byFm⊕FmF_{m}\oplus F_{m}on the Type III cell and byFmF_{m}on the
Type II companion) is a congruence carrying the present cells to those of the earlier
sections. The two normalizations therefore define congruent pencils, with identical
scalar densities and lying in the same symmetric-scalability class; we adopti+j=m+2i+j=m+2only because it makes the gauge phases in §8.2telescope cleanly.

Fixm≥2m\geq 2andβ∈ℂ∖ℝ\beta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}, and writeβ=ρ​ei​θ\beta=\rho e^{i\theta}withρ=|β|>0\rho=|\beta|>0. PutH:=β​F+GH:=\beta F+G, soH∗=β¯​F+GH^{*}=\overline{\beta}F+G. TheType III
cellis the Hermitian binary pencilA=A1​x1+A2​x2A=A_{1}x_{1}+A_{2}x_{2}onℂ2​m\mathbb{C}^{2m}withA1=(0FF0)=F2​m,A2=(0HH∗0),A_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&F\\
F&0\end{pmatrix}=F_{2m},\qquad A_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&H\\
H^{*}&0\end{pmatrix},

and covariance mapηIII​(B)=A1​B​A1+A2​B​A2\eta_{\mathrm{III}}(B)=A_{1}BA_{1}+A_{2}BA_{2},B∈M2​m​(ℂ)B\in M_{2m}(\mathbb{C}).
Its companion is the size-mmType II map withrealparameterρ\rho,ηII,ρ​(Y)=F​Y​F+K​Y​K,K:=ρ​F+G.\eta_{\mathrm{II},\rho}(Y)=FYF+KYK,\qquad K:=\rho F+G.

## §8.2  Spectral coincidence

## Theorem 8.3(Type III reduces to Type II with parameter|β||\beta|).

The matrix semicircular elements ofηIII\eta_{\mathrm{III}}and ofηII,ρ\eta_{\mathrm{II},\rho}(ρ=|β|\rho=|\beta|) have the same scalar distribution:
their normalized scalar Cauchy transforms agree onℂ+\mathbb{C}^{+}, and hencefIII,β​(x)=fII,|β|​(x)for a.e.​x∈ℝ.f_{\mathrm{III},\beta}(x)=f_{\mathrm{II},|\beta|}(x)\qquad\text{for a.e.\ }x\in\mathbb{R}.

## Proof.

LetW=W​(u)W=W(u)be the unique accretive solution ofW−1=u​I2​m+ηIII​(W)W^{-1}=uI_{2m}+\eta_{\mathrm{III}}(W),Re​u>0\mathrm{Re}\,u>0; recallW​(u)=i​𝒢​(i​u)W(u)=i\mathcal{G}(iu),
sotr2​m⁡W​(u)\operatorname{tr}_{2m}W(u)is the normalized scalar Cauchy transform ofμIII,β\mu_{\mathrm{III},\beta}ati​uiu.

Step 1:WWis block diagonal.LetJ=Im⊕(−Im)J=I_{m}\oplus(-I_{m}). TheAkA_{k}are block off-diagonal, soJ​Ak​J=−AkJA_{k}J=-A_{k}andηIII​(J​B​J)=J​ηIII​(B)​J\eta_{\mathrm{III}}(JBJ)=J\eta_{\mathrm{III}}(B)J; asJ​(u​I)​J=u​IJ(uI)J=uI, the matrixJ​W​JJWJis also accretive, so by uniquenessW=JWJ=:X⊕VW=JWJ=:X\oplus V, withX−1=u​I+F​V​F+H​V​H∗,V−1=u​I+F​X​F+H∗​X​H.X^{-1}=uI+FVF+HVH^{*},\qquad V^{-1}=uI+FXF+H^{*}XH.

Step 2: the gauge removes the phase ofβ\beta.LetQ=diag⁡(q1,…,qm)Q=\operatorname{diag}(q_{1},\dots,q_{m}),qj=exp⁡(−i​(j−m+12)​θ)q_{j}=\exp\!\big(\!-i(j-\tfrac{m+1}{2})\theta\big),
and𝒬:=Q⊕Q¯\mathcal{Q}:=Q\oplus\overline{Q}(unitary). SinceFFis supported oni+j=m+1i+j=m+1andGGoni+j=m+2i+j=m+2, the phases telescope:Q∗​F​Q¯=F,Q∗​G​Q¯=ei​θ​G⟹Q∗​H​Q¯=ei​θ​K,QT​H∗​Q=e−i​θ​K.Q^{*}F\overline{Q}=F,\quad Q^{*}G\overline{Q}=e^{i\theta}G\ \Longrightarrow\ Q^{*}H\overline{Q}=e^{i\theta}K,\quad Q^{T}H^{*}Q=e^{-i\theta}K.

Conjugating the equation by𝒬∗​(⋅)​𝒬\mathcal{Q}^{*}(\cdot)\mathcal{Q}and writingW^:=𝒬∗​W​𝒬\widehat{W}:=\mathcal{Q}^{*}W\mathcal{Q}(still block diagonal) givesW^−1=u​I+∑k(𝒬∗​Ak​𝒬)​W^​(𝒬∗​Ak​𝒬)\widehat{W}^{-1}=uI+\sum_{k}(\mathcal{Q}^{*}A_{k}\mathcal{Q})\,\widehat{W}\,(\mathcal{Q}^{*}A_{k}\mathcal{Q}),
where𝒬∗​A1​𝒬=A^1:=(0FF0)\mathcal{Q}^{*}A_{1}\mathcal{Q}=\widehat{A}_{1}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&F\\
F&0\end{pmatrix}and𝒬∗​A2​𝒬=(0ei​θ​Ke−i​θ​K0)\mathcal{Q}^{*}A_{2}\mathcal{Q}=\begin{pmatrix}0&e^{i\theta}K\\
e^{-i\theta}K&0\end{pmatrix}.
On a block-diagonal argument the two phases cancel, so this last term equalsA^2​W^​A^2\widehat{A}_{2}\widehat{W}\widehat{A}_{2}withA^2:=(0KK0)\widehat{A}_{2}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&K\\
K&0\end{pmatrix}. HenceW^\widehat{W}solves thereal-parameter equationW^−1=u​I+η^​(W^)\widehat{W}^{-1}=uI+\widehat{\eta}(\widehat{W}),η^​(B):=A^1​B​A^1+A^2​B​A^2\widehat{\eta}(B):=\widehat{A}_{1}B\widehat{A}_{1}+\widehat{A}_{2}B\widehat{A}_{2}.

Step 3: the swap collapses the two blocks.AsF,KF,Kare real symmetric, the block swapR=(0ImIm0)R=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{m}\\
I_{m}&0\end{pmatrix}satisfiesR​A^k​R=A^kR\widehat{A}_{k}R=\widehat{A}_{k}, soRRcommutes withη^\widehat{\eta}and withu​IuI.
By uniqueness,R​W^​R=W^R\widehat{W}R=\widehat{W}, i.e. the two diagonal blocks coincide,W^=Y⊕Y\widehat{W}=Y\oplus Y. The surviving block satisfiesY−1=u​I+F​Y​F+K​Y​K=u​I+ηII,ρ​(Y)Y^{-1}=uI+FYF+KYK=uI+\eta_{\mathrm{II},\rho}(Y), the Type II equation; by

The normalized scalar Cauchy transforms agree onℂ+\mathbb{C}^{+}; as each is the Cauchy
transform of a compactly supported probability measure, the scalar measures

∎

Thus the size-44cell of Example8.2(β=i\beta=i) has exactly the
scalar density of the size-22Type II cell with parameter11.

## Corollary 8.4(Leading singularity of a Type III cell).

Forβ∈ℂ∖ℝ\beta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R},fIII,β​(x)\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{III},\beta}(x)=Cβ,m​|x|−m−1m+1+o​(|x|−m−1m+1),x→0,\displaystyle=C_{\beta,m}\,|x|^{-\frac{m-1}{m+1}}+o\!\big(|x|^{-\frac{m-1}{m+1}}\big),\quad x\to 0,Cβ,m\displaystyle C_{\beta,m}=1m​π​sin⁡(πm+1)​(1+|β|2)−2​m−1m+1.\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m\pi}\sin\!\Big(\tfrac{\pi}{m+1}\Big)\big(1+|\beta|^{2}\big)^{-\frac{2m-1}{m+1}}.

## Proof.

By Theorem8.3the density equals that of the
size-mmType II cell with parameterα=|β|\alpha=|\beta|; apply
Theorem6.1withn=mn=m,α=|β|\alpha=|\beta|, and use1−3​mm+1=−2​m−1m+11-\tfrac{3m}{m+1}=-\tfrac{2m-1}{m+1}.
∎

In particular the size-2​m2mType III cell carries the singularity exponent of a
size-mmType II cell,m−1m+1\tfrac{m-1}{m+1}— not2​m−12​m+1\tfrac{2m-1}{2m+1}. The full
solution isW​(u)=𝒬​(Y⊕Y)​𝒬∗=Q​Y​Q∗⊕Q¯​Y​QTW(u)=\mathcal{Q}\,(Y\oplus Y)\,\mathcal{Q}^{*}=QYQ^{*}\oplus\overline{Q}\,Y\,Q^{T}withY=WII,ρ​(u)Y=W_{\mathrm{II},\rho}(u).

## §8.3  The covariance maps are not symmetrically scalable

Recall (Definition1.1) thatηA,ηB\eta_{A},\eta_{B}are symmetrically
scalable ifηB​(X)=b​ηA​(b∗​X​b)​b∗\eta_{B}(X)=b\,\eta_{A}(b^{*}Xb)\,b^{*}for some invertiblebb.
The
separating invariant is the real span of the Hermitian Kraus matrices; we first verify
this span is a property of themap. Write a self-adjoint covariance map asη​(X)=∑i=1rAi​X​Ai\eta(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}A_{i}XA_{i}withAi=Ai∗A_{i}=A_{i}^{*}, and call the familyminimalif
theAiA_{i}are linearly independent overℂ\mathbb{C}(sorris the Choi rank).

## Lemma 8.5(Unitary freedom of minimal Kraus families).

LetΦ​(X)=∑i=1rAi​X​Ai∗=∑j=1sMj​X​Mj∗\Phi(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}A_{i}XA_{i}^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^{s}M_{j}XM_{j}^{*}for allX∈Mn​(ℂ)X\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C}),
and suppose{Ai}\{A_{i}\}and{Mj}\{M_{j}\}are each linearly independent overℂ\mathbb{C}. Thenr=sr=s, and there is a unitaryw=(wi​j)∈U​(r)w=(w_{ij})\in U(r)withAi=∑jwi​j​MjA_{i}=\sum_{j}w_{ij}M_{j}.

## Proof.

Let♭:Mn​(ℂ)→ℂn2\flat\colon M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathbb{C}^{n^{2}},♭​(A):=∑kek⊗A​ek\flat(A):=\sum_{k}e_{k}\otimes Ae_{k}; this is a
linear isomorphism. WritingEk​l=ek​el∗E_{kl}=e_{k}e_{l}^{*}andAi​Ek​l​Ai∗=(Ai​ek)​(Ai​el)∗A_{i}E_{kl}A_{i}^{*}=(A_{i}e_{k})(A_{i}e_{l})^{*},
the Choi matrix ofΦ\PhisatisfiesCΦ:=∑k,lEk​l⊗Φ​(Ek​l)=∑i♭​(Ai)​♭​(Ai)∗=∑j♭​(Mj)​♭​(Mj)∗.C_{\Phi}:=\sum_{k,l}E_{kl}\otimes\Phi(E_{kl})=\sum_{i}\flat(A_{i})\,\flat(A_{i})^{*}=\sum_{j}\flat(M_{j})\,\flat(M_{j})^{*}.

Since♭\flatis an isomorphism, linear independence of the families makes𝖠,𝖡\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B}of full column rank; hencer=rank⁡CΦ=sr=\operatorname{rank}C_{\Phi}=s, andrange⁡𝖠=range⁡CΦ=range⁡𝖡\operatorname{range}\mathsf{A}=\operatorname{range}C_{\Phi}=\operatorname{range}\mathsf{B}.
As𝖡\mathsf{B}is injective, each column of𝖠\mathsf{A}lies inrange⁡𝖡\operatorname{range}\mathsf{B}and so𝖠=𝖡​wT\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{B}\,w^{T}for a uniquewT∈Mr​(ℂ)w^{T}\in M_{r}(\mathbb{C}). Then𝖡​(wT​(wT)∗−I)​𝖡∗=0\mathsf{B}(w^{T}(w^{T})^{*}-I)\mathsf{B}^{*}=0, and injectivity of𝖡\mathsf{B}forceswT​(wT)∗=Iw^{T}(w^{T})^{*}=I, i.e.w∈U​(r)w\in U(r). Reading off columns,♭​(Ai)=∑jwi​j​♭​(Mj)=♭​(∑jwi​j​Mj)\flat(A_{i})=\sum_{j}w_{ij}\,\flat(M_{j})=\flat\!\big(\sum_{j}w_{ij}M_{j}\big), and injectivity of♭\flatgivesAi=∑jwi​j​MjA_{i}=\sum_{j}w_{ij}M_{j}.
∎

## Lemma 8.6(The Hermitian Kraus plane is well defined).

If{Ai}i=1r\{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r}and{Mj}j=1r\{M_{j}\}_{j=1}^{r}are two minimal Hermitian Kraus families for
the same mapη\eta, thenspanℝ⁡{Ai}=spanℝ⁡{Mj}\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{A_{i}\}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}}\{M_{j}\}.
We write𝒦η,sa⊆Herm⁡(n)\mathcal{K}_{\eta,\mathrm{sa}}\subseteq\operatorname{Herm}(n)for this subspace.

adjoints and usingAi∗=AiA_{i}^{*}=A_{i},Mj∗=MjM_{j}^{*}=M_{j}gives∑jwi​j​Mj=∑jwi​j¯​Mj\sum_{j}w_{ij}M_{j}=\sum_{j}\overline{w_{ij}}\,M_{j}; linear independence of{Mj}\{M_{j}\}forceswi​j∈ℝw_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}, sowwis real orthogonal and the real spans coincide.
∎

eachb​Ai​b∗bA_{i}b^{*}Hermitian (asAi=Ai∗A_{i}=A_{i}^{*}, so(b​Ai​b∗)∗=b​Ai∗​b∗=b​Ai​b∗(bA_{i}b^{*})^{*}=bA_{i}^{*}b^{*}=bA_{i}b^{*}) and

We call a plane satisfying this conditionnonsingular. (This is regularity, not
sign-definiteness: every nonzero element of the Type III plane is in fact an indefinite
Hermitian matrix, with symmetric spectrum.)

## Theorem 8.10(Type III is not symmetrically scalable to real Type II sums).

Forβ∈ℂ∖ℝ\beta\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}and anym≥2m\geq 2,ηIII\eta_{\mathrm{III}}is not symmetrically

law).

∎

## §9  Classification of singularities for Hermitian binary pencils

We collect the cell computations of §§5–8into a single statement. For a cell,
define itseffective chain lengthn∗n^{*}:n∗=nn^{*}=nfor aType​I\mathrm{Type\ I}orType​II\mathrm{Type\ II}cell of sizenn, andn∗=mn^{*}=mfor aType​III\mathrm{Type\ III}cell of size2​m2m. (ForType​III\mathrm{Type\ III}this is the chain length of theType​II\mathrm{Type\ II}cell to which its scalar density reduces,
Corollary8.4.) A cell is LR-semisimple iffn∗=1n^{*}=1.

## Theorem 9.1(Leading singularity of a regular Hermitian binary pencil).

LetA=A1​x1+A2​x2A=A_{1}x_{1}+A_{2}x_{2}be a regular Hermitian binary pencil, with Lancaster–Rodman
cellsB(1),…,B(k)B^{(1)},\dots,B^{(k)}of effective chain lengthsn1∗,…,nk∗n_{1}^{*},\dots,n_{k}^{*}, and setN∗:=maxj⁡nj∗N^{*}:=\max_{j}n_{j}^{*}. ThenμSA\mu_{S_{A}}has no atom at0and leading Puiseux exponent
there equal to−α∗-\alpha_{*}, whereα∗=N∗−1N∗+1,i.e.fA​(x)=C​|x|−N∗−1N∗+1+o​(|x|−N∗−1N∗+1),x→0,C>0.\alpha_{*}=\frac{N^{*}-1}{N^{*}+1},\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad f_{A}(x)=C\,|x|^{-\frac{N^{*}-1}{N^{*}+1}}+o\!\big(|x|^{-\frac{N^{*}-1}{N^{*}+1}}\big),\quad x\to 0,\ C>0.

In particularμSA\mu_{S_{A}}is regular at0(α∗=0\alpha_{*}=0) iffN∗=1N^{*}=1, i.e. iffAAis LR-semisimple.

## Proof.

SinceAAis regular, the matrix semicircleSAS_{A}is full
(Proposition4.3), so by Proposition4.8α∗=maxj⁡αj\alpha_{*}=\max_{j}\alpha_{j}, whereαj\alpha_{j}is the singularity exponent of the cellB(j)B^{(j)}(congruence-invariance
and the positive, hence cancellation-free, direct-sum law). The cell exponents are(n−1)/(n+1)(n-1)/(n+1)forType​I\mathrm{Type\ I}andType​II\mathrm{Type\ II}cells of sizenn(Theorems5.1and6.1)
and(m−1)/(m+1)(m-1)/(m+1)forType​III\mathrm{Type\ III}cells of size2​m2m(Corollary8.4); in every caseαj=(nj∗−1)/(nj∗+1)\alpha_{j}=(n_{j}^{*}-1)/(n_{j}^{*}+1).
Sinceg​(n)=n−1n+1=1−2n+1g(n)=\frac{n-1}{n+1}=1-\frac{2}{n+1}is strictly increasing onn≥1n\geq 1,maxj⁡αj=g​(maxj⁡nj∗)=g​(N∗)\max_{j}\alpha_{j}=g(\max_{j}n_{j}^{*})=g(N^{*}). The semisimplicity equivalence isN∗=1⇔N^{*}=1\iffeverynj∗=1⇔n_{j}^{*}=1\iffevery cell is LR-semisimple.
∎

## Remark 9.2(WhatN∗N^{*}measures: deviation from semisimplicity).

The invariantN∗N^{*}has a coordinate-free meaning in the indefinite geometry from
which the Lancaster–Rodman form arises(Gohberget al.,2005, Thm. 5.1.1). SinceAAis
regular,H:=A1+s​A2H:=A_{1}+sA_{2}is invertible for all but finitely many realss(takes=0s=0whenA1A_{1}is invertible); putT:=H−1​A2T:=H^{-1}A_{2}. ThenH​T=A2HT=A_{2}is Hermitian, soTTisHH-selfadjoint, i.e. self-adjoint for the indefinite inner product[x,y]:=y∗​H​x[x,y]:=y^{*}Hx, and the Lancaster–Rodman cells are precisely the Jordan blocks ofTTin this metric(Gohberget al.,2005, Thm. 5.1.1, Prop. 4.2.3)— equivalently the blocks of
the pair canonical formLancaster and Rodman (2005)viaT=H−1​A2T=H^{-1}A_{2}— the sign
characteristic recording the sign of[⋅,⋅][\cdot,\cdot]on each block; the sizesnj∗n_{j}^{*}are independent of the (generic)ss.

For a self-adjoint operator in adefinitemetric the spectral theorem forbids
Jordan blocks; a block of sizen>1n>1occurs only because[⋅,⋅][\cdot,\cdot]degenerates
along the corresponding root space. On a cell of sizennthe metric is the reversalH=±FnH=\pm F_{n}(Gohberget al.,2005, Ex. 4.2.1), so the uniqueTT-invariant flag⟨v1,…,vk⟩\langle v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\ranglehas[vi,vj]=±δi+j,n+1[v_{i},v_{j}]=\pm\delta_{i+j,\,n+1}and is totally[⋅,⋅][\cdot,\cdot]-neutral iff2​k≤n2k\leq n; hence the largest neutralTT-invariant subspace
inside the cell has dimension⌊n/2⌋\lfloor n/2\rfloor(cf. the neutral-subspace bound(Gohberget al.,2005, Thm. 2.3.4)). For aType​III\mathrm{Type\ III}cell (dim2​m\dim 2m, a non-real eigenvalue
pair, whose root subspaces are automatically[⋅,⋅][\cdot,\cdot]-neutral(Gohberget al.,2005, Thm. 5.1.1)) this gives⌊2​m/2⌋=m\lfloor 2m/2\rfloor=m. Thus, up to the parity
correction,N∗N^{*}is twice the largest dimension of aTT-invariant subspace on whichHHvanishes identically:N∗N^{*}measures how deeply the eigendirections of the
pencil sink into the neutral cone of the indefinite metric.LR-semisimplicity
(N∗=1N^{*}=1) is exactly the absence of any nonzeroTT-invariant neutral subspace —
nondegeneracy of the metric on every eigendirection.

Writingd:=N∗−1d:=N^{*}-1for the nilpotent depth of the largest block — thedefect of
semisimplicity, algebraic minus geometric multiplicity — this readsα∗=N∗−1N∗+1=dd+2.\alpha_{*}=\frac{N^{*}-1}{N^{*}+1}=\frac{d}{d+2}.

The singularity exponent is the defect filtered through the quadratic term of Speicher’s
equationz​G=I+η​(G)​GzG=I+\eta(G)G: the “+2+2” is the degree that term adds to the characteristic
equation over the depthdd, interpolating fromα∗=0\alpha_{*}=0atd=0d=0(smooth) toα∗→1\alpha_{*}\to 1asd→∞d\to\infty.

## Remark 9.3(The exponent classification is strictly coarser).

The leading exponent depends on the cells only throughN∗=maxj⁡nj∗N^{*}=\max_{j}n_{j}^{*}— not on
their types, parameters, or sign characteristic, and not on whether a given chain
length is realized by aType​II\mathrm{Type\ II}cell of sizemmor aType​III\mathrm{Type\ III}cell of size2​m2m.
Thus distinct LR data can give the same scalar density. The sharpest instance is
§8:ηII,|β|\eta_{\mathrm{II},|\beta|}andηIII,β\eta_{\mathrm{III},\beta}(and direct sums
of them) have identical scalar densities yet are not symmetrically scalable
(Theorem8.10). When several cells attainN∗N^{*}, the
constantCCis the correspondingnj∗/nn_{j}^{*}/n-weighted sum of the cell constants (which
depend on theType​II\mathrm{Type\ II}/Type​III\mathrm{Type\ III}parameter through1+α21+\alpha^{2},1+|β|21+|\beta|^{2}); the
sum is positive, with no cancellation.

## Appendix BTauberian lemmas for Poisson asymptotics

## §B.1  Proof of the Puiseux-to-Poisson lemma

## Proof.

We may assume without loss of generality that the Poisson integral
converges for all smallε>0\varepsilon>0; in the application,ffis a spectral density satisfying∫ℝf<∞\int_{\mathbb{R}}f<\infty.

Step 1: Reduction to half-lines.Write1π​∫ℝε​f​(x)x2+ε2​𝑑x=I+​(ε)+I−​(ε),I±​(ε)=1π​∫0∞ε​f​(±x)x2+ε2​𝑑x.\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\varepsilon\,f(x)}{x^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}}\,dx\;=\;I_{+}(\varepsilon)+I_{-}(\varepsilon),\qquad I_{\pm}(\varepsilon)\;=\;\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\varepsilon\,f(\pm x)}{x^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}}\,dx.

We treatI+I_{+}; the analysis ofI−I_{-}is identical withc−c_{-}replacingc+c_{+}.

Step 2: Rescaling.The substitutionx=ε​ux=\varepsilon ugivesI+​(ε)=1π​∫0∞f​(ε​u)u2+1​𝑑u.I_{+}(\varepsilon)\;=\;\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{f(\varepsilon u)}{u^{2}+1}\,du.

Fixη>0\eta>0small enough that|f​(x)−c+​x−α|≤C​x−α+δ|f(x)-c_{+}x^{-\alpha}|\leq Cx^{-\alpha+\delta}for allx∈(0,η]x\in(0,\eta], and splitI+​(ε)=J1​(ε)+J2​(ε),J1=1π​∫0η/εf​(ε​u)u2+1​𝑑u,J2=1π​∫η/ε∞f​(ε​u)u2+1​𝑑u.I_{+}(\varepsilon)=J_{1}(\varepsilon)+J_{2}(\varepsilon),\qquad J_{1}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\eta/\varepsilon}\frac{f(\varepsilon u)}{u^{2}+1}\,du,\quad J_{2}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\eta/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\frac{f(\varepsilon u)}{u^{2}+1}\,du.

Step 3: Tail estimate.Returning to the original variable,J2​(ε)=1π​∫η∞ε​f​(x)x2+ε2​𝑑x≤επ​η2​∫η∞f​(x)​𝑑x=O​(ε).J_{2}(\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\eta}^{\infty}\frac{\varepsilon\,f(x)}{x^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}}\,dx\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\pi\eta^{2}}\int_{\eta}^{\infty}f(x)\,dx=O(\varepsilon).

Step 4: Main term.On the intervalu∈(0,η/ε]u\in(0,\,\eta/\varepsilon]we haveε​u∈(0,η]\varepsilon u\in(0,\eta], so the asymptotic hypothesis applies:f​(ε​u)=c+​(ε​u)−α+r​(ε​u),|r​(ε​u)|≤C​(ε​u)−α+δ.f(\varepsilon u)=c_{+}\,(\varepsilon u)^{-\alpha}+r(\varepsilon u),\qquad|r(\varepsilon u)|\leq C\,(\varepsilon u)^{-\alpha+\delta}.

ThusJ1=M+RJ_{1}=M+RwhereM=c+​ε−απ​∫0η/εu−αu2+1​𝑑u,|R|≤C​ε−α+δπ​∫0η/εu−α+δu2+1​𝑑u.M=\frac{c_{+}\,\varepsilon^{-\alpha}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\eta/\varepsilon}\frac{u^{-\alpha}}{u^{2}+1}\,du,\qquad|R|\leq\frac{C\,\varepsilon^{-\alpha+\delta}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\eta/\varepsilon}\frac{u^{-\alpha+\delta}}{u^{2}+1}\,du.

Evaluation ofMM.The beta-integral identity (substitutionu2=tu^{2}=t, then the
reflection formula) gives(77)∫0∞u−αu2+1​𝑑u=12​B​(1−α2,1+α2)=π2​sec⁡π​α2,α∈[0,1).\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{-\alpha}}{u^{2}+1}\,du=\frac{1}{2}\,B\!\Bigl(\frac{1-\alpha}{2},\,\frac{1+\alpha}{2}\Bigr)=\frac{\pi}{2}\,\sec\frac{\pi\alpha}{2},\qquad\alpha\in[0,1).

The complementary tail satisfies∫η/ε∞u−αu2+1​𝑑u≤∫η/ε∞u−α−2​𝑑u=(ε/η)1+α1+α=O​(ε1+α),\int_{\eta/\varepsilon}^{\infty}\frac{u^{-\alpha}}{u^{2}+1}\,du\leq\int_{\eta/\varepsilon}^{\infty}u^{-\alpha-2}\,du=\frac{(\varepsilon/\eta)^{1+\alpha}}{1+\alpha}=O(\varepsilon^{1+\alpha}),

soM=c+2​sec⁡π​α2​ε−α+O​(ε).M=\frac{c_{+}}{2}\,\sec\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\;\varepsilon^{-\alpha}+O(\varepsilon).

Bound onRR.Since−α+δ>−1-\alpha+\delta>-1(asα<1\alpha<1), the integral∫0∞u−α+δ/(u2+1)​𝑑u\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{-\alpha+\delta}/(u^{2}+1)\,duconverges
wheneverδ<1+α\delta<1+\alpha, givingR=O​(ε−α+δ)R=O(\varepsilon^{-\alpha+\delta}).

Step 5: Assembly.The three error contributions are:

the beta-integral completionO​(ε)O(\varepsilon)from Step 4,
and the remainderRR.
For the remainder:
sinceδ<1+α\delta<1+\alphaby assumption, the integral∫0∞u−α+δ/(u2+1)​𝑑u\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{-\alpha+\delta}/(u^{2}+1)\,duconverges, givingR=O​(ε−α+δ)R=O(\varepsilon^{-\alpha+\delta}).

The identical argument givesI−​(ε)=c−2​sec⁡π​α2​ε−α+O​(ε−α+δ)I_{-}(\varepsilon)=\frac{c_{-}}{2}\sec\frac{\pi\alpha}{2}\,\varepsilon^{-\alpha}+O(\varepsilon^{-\alpha+\delta}).

∎

it satisfiesP​(z,H)=0P(z,H)=0for a nontrivial polynomialPPwith real coefficients.

wherez1/mz^{1/m}denotes the branch analytic inℂ+\mathbb{C}^{+}withz1/m>0z^{1/m}>0forz>0z>0.

The Stieltjes inversion formulaf​(x)=−1π​Im​H​(x+i​0+)f(x)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\,\mathrm{Im}\,\,H(x+i0^{+})translates (78) into Puiseux expansions
offfon each side of the origin.
Forx>0x>0:zk/m=xk/mz^{k/m}=x^{k/m}is real, sof​(x)=−1π​∑k(Im​ck)​xk/mf(x)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{k}(\mathrm{Im}\,\,c_{k})\,x^{k/m}.

Forx<0x<0:z=|x|​ei​πz=|x|\,e^{i\pi}giveszk/m=|x|k/m​ei​π​k/mz^{k/m}=|x|^{k/m}\,e^{i\pi k/m}, sof​(−x)=−1π​∑k|ck|​xk/m​sin⁡(π​k/m+arg⁡ck)f(-x)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\sum_{k}|c_{k}|\,x^{k/m}\,\sin(\pi k/m+\arg c_{k})

forx>0x>0small.
In both cases the exponents are rational numbers of the
formβ=k/m\beta=k/m.

The constraintβ1>−1\beta_{1}>-1holds becausef≥0f\geq 0is locally
integrable (sinceμS\mu_{S}has no atom at0by hypothesis,∫−ηηf​(x)​𝑑x<∞\int_{-\eta}^{\eta}f(x)\,dx<\infty), which forces the leading
singular exponent to satisfyβ1>−1\beta_{1}>-1.

Part 2: Puiseux exponent determines
Poisson scaling.Letα∗=−β∗\alpha_{*}=-\beta_{*}whereβ∗=min⁡{βk:ak+≠0​or​ak−≠0}\beta_{*}=\min\{\beta_{k}:a_{k}^{+}\neq 0\text{ or }a_{k}^{-}\neq 0\}is the leading singular exponent.
Sincef≥0f\geq 0andf​(x)∼c+​x−α∗f(x)\sim c_{+}\,x^{-\alpha_{*}}asx↓0x\downarrow 0withc+≥0c_{+}\geq 0(resp.f​(−x)∼c−​x−α∗f(-x)\sim c_{-}\,x^{-\alpha_{*}}withc−≥0c_{-}\geq 0) andc++c−>0c_{+}+c_{-}>0,
Lemma3.4gives(79)1π​∫ℝε​f​(x)x2+ε2​𝑑x=c++c−2​sec⁡π​α∗2​ε−α∗+O​(ε−α∗+δ).\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\varepsilon\,f(x)}{x^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}}\,dx\;=\;\frac{c_{+}+c_{-}}{2}\,\sec\frac{\pi\alpha_{*}}{2}\;\varepsilon^{-\alpha_{*}}+O(\varepsilon^{-\alpha_{*}+\delta}).

SinceμS\mu_{S}has no atom at0, its singular part (if any)
is supported at distance at leastr>0r>0from the origin, and
its Poisson contribution is1π​∫ℝεx2+ε2​𝑑μSsing​(x)≤επ​r2​μSsing​(ℝ)=O​(ε).\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\varepsilon}{x^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}}\,d\mu_{S}^{\mathrm{sing}}(x)\;\leq\;\frac{\varepsilon}{\pi r^{2}}\,\mu_{S}^{\mathrm{sing}}(\mathbb{R})\;=\;O(\varepsilon).

Asα∗≥0\alpha_{*}\geq 0, theO​(ε)O(\varepsilon)term is absorbed by (79), giving𝒫S​(ε)≍ε−α∗\mathcal{P}_{S}(\varepsilon)\asymp\varepsilon^{-\alpha_{*}}.

Part 3: Converse.Part 2 establishes𝒫S​(ε)≍ε−α∗\mathcal{P}_{S}(\varepsilon)\asymp\varepsilon^{-\alpha_{*}}unconditionally (given the Puiseux expansion).
If simultaneously𝒫S​(ε)≍ε−α\mathcal{P}_{S}(\varepsilon)\asymp\varepsilon^{-\alpha},

If equality holds, thenLn​H=H​Ln,Un​H=H​Un,E∂​H=0,H​E∂=0.L_{n}H=HL_{n},\qquad U_{n}H=HU_{n},\qquad E_{\partial}H=0,\qquad HE_{\partial}=0.

Diagonal entries.LetRi:=Ri​iR_{i}:=R_{ii}.

we get, for1≤i≤n−11\leq i\leq n-1,(98)Ri=ri2​(𝟏i>1​ri−1​pi−12+ri+1​pi2)−2​a​pi−b​ri+1.R_{i}=r_{i}^{2}\left(\mathbf{1}_{i>1}\,r_{i-1}p_{i-1}^{2}+r_{i+1}p_{i}^{2}\right)-2ap_{i}-br_{i+1}.
